X.\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{3}X,a:\bullet$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\vskip-0.99814pt\hbox{\hskip 106.72202pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to676.77621pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.24812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize{{[{typ-recv}]}}}}\vskip-0.99812pt\hbox{\hskip 247.66434pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\emptyset\begin{array}[t]{@{}r@{}l@{}}{}\vdash&x(a);y(b);\mu X(x,y);x(a^{\prime});y(b^{\prime});X\langle x,y\rangle\\
{}::&x:\underbrace{\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{0}\mu X.\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{2}X}_{\unfold^{2}(\mu X.\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{0}X)},y:\underbrace{\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{1}\mu X.\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{3}X}_{\unfold^{2}(\mu X.\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{1}X)}\end{array}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\end{array}

Clearly, the typing of the unfolded process is not the same as the initial type, but they are equal up to unfolding of types.
Note that the application of Rule [
typ-rec
] unfolds the types with a common lifter of 4, because it needs to be larger than the top priority in the types before unfolding which is 3.

Hence, type preservation holds
up to unfolding
.
To formalize this, we define the relation
(\vdash P::\Gamma)\mathrel{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{1}[.8]{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\lesssim$}}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
, which says that
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
are equal up to (un)folding of recursive types consistent with the typing of
P
under
\Gamma
:

{defi}

We define an asymmetric relation between a typed process
(\Omega\vdash P::\Gamma)
and a typing context
\Gamma^{\prime}
, denoted
(\Omega\vdash P::\Gamma)\mathrel{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{1}[.8]{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\lesssim$}}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
.
The relation is defined by the inference rules in
Figure

6
, where each rule implicitly requires that
\Omega\vdash P::\Gamma
is a valid typing derivation by the rules in
Figure

4
.

We write
(\Omega\vdash P::\Gamma)\cong(\Omega^{\prime}\vdash Q::\Gamma^{\prime})
if
(\Omega\vdash P::\Gamma)\mathrel{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{1}[.8]{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\lesssim$}}}}\Gamma^{\prime}
and
(\Omega^{\prime}\vdash Q::\Gamma^{\prime})\mathrel{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{1}[.8]{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\lesssim$}}}}\Gamma
.

\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{unf-fold}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$(\Omega\vdash P\big\{\big(\mu X(\tilde{y});P\{\tilde{y}/\tilde{z}\}\big)/X\langle\tilde{y}\rangle\big\}::\tilde{z}:\tilde{U})\mathrel{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{1}[.8]{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\lesssim$}}}}\tilde{z}:\tilde{U^{\prime}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\forall U^{\prime}_{i}\in\tilde{U^{\prime}};~U^{\prime}_{i}=\unfold^{t}(\mu X.A^{\prime}_{i})$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to385.02116pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 75.16096pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$(\Omega\vdash P\big\{\big(\mu X(\tilde{y});P\{\tilde{y}/\tilde{z}\}\big)/X\langle\tilde{y}\rangle\big\}::\tilde{z}:\tilde{U})\mathrel{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{1}[.8]{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\lesssim$}}}}\tilde{z}:\widetilde{\mu X.A^{\prime}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{unf-unf}]}}}}\\

Figure 6.
Inference rules for
Section

3.3
.

The most important rules of
Figure

6
are Rules [
unf-unf
] and [
unf-fold
] (above the line), as they relate unfolding and folding; the other rule (below the line) follow the typing rules in
Figure

4
.
Note that the rules in
Figure

6
require no priority requirements, as they are covered by the implicit validity of the derivation of
\Omega\vdash P::\Gamma
.

Proposition 5
.

If (i)
(\Omega_{1}\vdash P_{1}::\Gamma_{1})\cong(\Omega_{2}\vdash P_{2}::\Gamma_{2})
and (ii)
(\Omega_{2}\vdash P_{2}::\Gamma_{2})\cong(\Omega_{3}\vdash P_{3}::\Gamma_{3})
, then
(\Omega_{1}\vdash P_{1}::\Gamma_{1})\cong(\Omega_{3}\vdash P_{3}::\Gamma_{3})
.

Proof 3.3

(Proof (Sketch))
.

Since
\cong
is reflexive by definition, it suffices to show that (i)
(\Omega_{1}\vdash P_{1}::\Gamma_{1})\mathrel{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{1}[.8]{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\lesssim$}}}}\Gamma_{2}
and (ii)
(\Omega_{2}\vdash P_{2}::\Gamma_{2})\mathrel{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{1}[.8]{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\lesssim$}}}}\Gamma_{3}
imply
(\Omega_{1}\vdash P_{1}::\Gamma_{1})\mathrel{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{1}[.8]{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\lesssim$}}}}\Gamma_{3}
.
Assumptions (i) and (ii) relate
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\Gamma_{3}
by applications of Rules
[
unf-fold
]
and
[
unf-unf
]
.
Clearly, the relation between
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{3}
can then be derived directly by combining the applications of these rules.

Subject congruence additionally requires the following property of typing derivations involving recursion variables:

Proposition 6
.

Suppose given a process
P
and a derivation
\pi
of
\Omega,X:{(A_{l})}_{l\in L}\vdash P::\Gamma
.
In every step in
\pi
, the assignment
X:{(A_{l})}_{l\in L}
in the recursive context is not modified nor removed, and no other assignments on the recursion variable
X
are added.

Proof 3.4

(Proof (Sketch))
.

By induction on the height of the derivation
\pi
: no typing rule eliminates assignments from the recursive context, and no typing rule changes the types assigned to a recursion variable in the recursive context.
Moreover, in our type system, any rule adding an assignment to a context implicitly assumes that the newly introduced name or recursion variable is not in the context yet.
Hence, an assignment on
X
cannot be added by any rule.

Now we can state and prove subject congruence, which holds up to the relation in
Section

3.3
:

Theorem 3.4

(Subject Congruence)
.

If
\Omega\vdash P::\Gamma
and
P\equiv Q
, then there exists
\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
\Omega\vdash Q::\Gamma^{\prime}
and
(\Omega\vdash P::\Gamma)\cong(\Omega\vdash Q::\Gamma^{\prime})
.

Proof 3.5
.

By induction on the derivation of
P\equiv Q
.
The cases for the structural rules follow from the IH directly.
All base cases are straightforward, except the cases of unfolding and folding.
We detail unfolding, where
P=\mu X(\tilde{z});P^{\prime}
and
Q=P^{\prime}\big\{\big(\mu X(\tilde{y});P^{\prime}\{\tilde{y}/\tilde{z}\}\big)/X\langle\tilde{y}\rangle\big\}
.
The other direction, folding, is similar.

By type inversion of Rule
[
typ-rec
]
,

\displaystyle\Omega

\displaystyle\vdash P::\Omega;\tilde{z}:\widetilde{\mu X.A}

\displaystyle\Omega,X:\tilde{A}

\displaystyle\vdash P^{\prime}::\tilde{z}:\tilde{U}

where there exists
t\in\mathbb{N}>\prtop(\widetilde{\mu X.A})
such that each

U_{i}=\unfold^{t}(\mu X.A_{i})=A_{i}\{\big(\mu X.{\uparrow^{\mkern-3.0mut}}A_{i}\big)/X\}.

Let
\nabla
denote the typing derivation of
P^{\prime}
.

P^{\prime}
may contain
m\geq 0
recursive calls
X\langle\tilde{y}\rangle
.
For each call, there is an application of Rule
[
typ-var
]
in
\nabla
.
We uniquely identify each such application of Rule
[
typ-var
]
as
\partial_{j}
for
0<j\leq m
.
Since
APCP
only allows tail recursion, these multiple recursive calls can only occur inside branches on names not in
\tilde{z}
, so the common lifter of each
\partial_{j}
must be
t
.

\displaystyle\partial_{j}\left\{\qquad\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 47.61197pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-0.99814pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to103.22395pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.24812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize{{[{typ-var}]}}}}\vskip-0.99812pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Omega^{\prime}_{j},X:\tilde{A}\vdash X\langle\tilde{y}_{j}\rangle::\tilde{y}_{j}:\widetilde{\mu X.{\uparrow^{\mkern-3.0mut}}A}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\end{array}\right.

where
\Omega^{\prime}_{j}\supseteq\Omega
, because rules applied in
\nabla
can only add assignments to the recursive context.
Recall that
\tilde{y}_{j}:\widetilde{\mu X.{\uparrow^{\mkern-3.0mut}}A}
is notation for
y_{j_{1}}:\mu X.{\uparrow^{\mkern-3.0mut}}A_{1},\ldots,y_{j_{k}}:\mu X.{\uparrow^{\mkern-3.0mut}}A_{k}
, as introduced in
Section

3.2
.

The unfolding of a recursive process replaces each recursive call by a copy of the recursive definition.
Hence, to find a typing derivation for
Q
we proceed as follows, for each
0<j\leq m
:

(1)

We obtain a derivation
\nabla^{\prime}_{j}
of
P^{\prime}\{\tilde{y}_{j}/\tilde{z}\}
from
\nabla
by substituting
\tilde{z}
for
\tilde{y}_{j}
(while avoiding capturing names) and by lifting all priorities by
t
(including the priorities of
X
in the recursive context of
\partial_{j}
).

(2)

We apply Rule
[
typ-rec
]
on
X
in the conclusion of
\nabla^{\prime}_{j}
, resulting in a new typing derivation
\partial^{\prime}_{j}
:

\displaystyle\partial^{\prime}_{j}\left\{\qquad\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 6.23067pt\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 48.50002pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\nabla^{\prime}_{j}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Omega^{\prime}_{j},X:\widetilde{{\uparrow^{\mkern-3.0mut}}A}\vdash P^{\prime}\{\tilde{y}_{j}/\tilde{z}\}::\tilde{y}_{j}:\widetilde{{\uparrow^{\mkern-3.0mut}}U}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\vskip-0.99814pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to130.00829pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.24812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize{{[{typ-rec}]}}}}\vskip-0.99812pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Omega^{\prime}_{j}\vdash\mu X(\tilde{y}_{j});P^{\prime}\{\tilde{y}_{j}/\tilde{z}\}::\tilde{y}_{j}:\widetilde{\mu X.{\uparrow^{\mkern-3.0mut}}A}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\end{array}\right.

For every
0<j\leq m
, the context of the conclusion of
\partial^{\prime}_{j}
coincides with the context of the conclusion of
\partial_{j}
, up to the assignment
X:\widetilde{{\uparrow^{\mkern-3.0mut}}A}
in the recursive context not present in
\partial^{\prime}_{j}
.
We intend to obtain from
\nabla
a new derivation
\nabla^{\prime}
by replacing each
\partial_{j}
with
\partial^{\prime}_{j}
.
By
6
, the fact that each
\partial^{\prime}_{j}
is missing the assignment to
X
in the recursive context does not influence the steps in
\nabla
.
Hence, we can indeed obtain such a derivation
\nabla^{\prime}
:

\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 15.95831pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\nabla^{\prime}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Omega\vdash\underbrace{P^{\prime}\big\{\big(\mu X(\tilde{y});P^{\prime}\{\tilde{y}/\tilde{z}\}\big)/X\langle\tilde{y}\rangle\big\}}_{Q}::\tilde{z}:\tilde{U}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\end{array}

where
\forall U_{i}\in\tilde{U}.~U_{i}=\unfold^{t}(A_{i})
.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}=\tilde{z}:\tilde{U}
.
We have
\Omega\vdash Q::\Gamma^{\prime}
.
To prove the thesis, we have to show that
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
are equal up to unfolding.
Following the typing rules applied in the derivation of
\Omega\vdash Q::\tilde{z}:\tilde{U}
, we have
(\Omega\vdash Q::\tilde{z}:\tilde{U})\mathrel{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{1}[.8]{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\lesssim$}}}}\tilde{z}:\tilde{U}
(cf.
Section

3.3
).
Then, by Rule
[
unf-fold
]
,
(\Omega\vdash Q::\tilde{z}:\tilde{U})\mathrel{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{1}[.8]{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\lesssim$}}}}\tilde{z}:\widetilde{\mu X.A}
.
Similarly, we have
(\Omega\vdash P::\tilde{z}:\widetilde{\mu X.A})\mathrel{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{1}[.8]{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\lesssim$}}}}\tilde{z}:\widetilde{\mu X.A}
, so by Rule
[
unf-unf
]
,
(\Omega\vdash P::\tilde{z}:\widetilde{\mu X.A})\mathrel{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{1}[.8]{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\lesssim$}}}}\tilde{z}:\tilde{U}
.
Hence,
(\Omega\vdash P::\Gamma)\cong(\Omega\vdash Q::\Gamma^{\prime})
, proving the thesis.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 63.44017pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-0.99814pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to134.88034pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.24812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize{{[{typ-send}]}}}}\vskip-0.99812pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash x[a,b]::x:A\mathbin{\otimes}^{\circ}B,a:\overline{A},b:\overline{B}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\hbox{\hskip 4.30554pt}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 73.39372pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash P::\Gamma,z:\overline{A},y^{\prime}:\overline{B}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-0.99814pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to229.2259pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.24812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize{{[{typ-recv}]}}}}\vskip-0.99812pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash y(z,y^{\prime});P::\Gamma,y:\overline{A}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\circ}\overline{B}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\vskip 0.00186pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-1.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to405.7189pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\hss}\raise 2.0pt\hbox to405.7189pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}}\lower 0.24812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize{{[{typ-par}]}}+{{[{typ-res}]}}}}\vskip 0.00188pt\hbox{\hskip 114.72705pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash(\bm{\nu}xy)(x[a,b]\mathbin{|}y(z,y^{\prime});P)::\Gamma,a:\overline{A},b:\overline{B}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\end{array}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\rightarrow}

\displaystyle\vdash P\{a/z,b/y^{\prime}\}::\Gamma,a:\overline{A},b:\overline{B}

Figure 7.

Example of subject reduction (cf.
8
) in Rule [
red-send-recv
].
The well typedness of the process before reduction allows us to infer its typing derivation, also giving us the typing of
P
.
Typing the process after reduction is then a matter of inductively substituting names in the typing derivation of
P
.

Having established subject congruence, we can prove that reduction preserves typing, i.e.,
subject reduction
:

Theorem 8

(Subject Reduction)
.

If
\Omega\vdash P::\Gamma
and
P\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q
, then there exists
\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
\Omega\vdash Q::\Gamma^{\prime}
and
{(\Omega\vdash P::\Gamma)\cong(\Omega\vdash Q::\Gamma^{\prime})}
.

Proof 3.6

(Proof (Sketch))
.

By induction on the derivation of
P\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q
(
Figure

2
(bottom)), we find a
\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
\Omega\vdash Q::\Gamma^{\prime}
and
(\Omega\vdash P::\Gamma)\cong(\Omega\vdash Q::\Gamma^{\prime})
.

The cases of Rules
[
red-res
]
and
[
red-par
]
follow directly from the IH.
Consider, e.g.,
(\bm{\nu}xy)(P\mathbin{|}R)\mathbin{\rightarrow}(\bm{\nu}xy)(Q\mathbin{|}R)
derived from
P\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q
.
By inversion of typing on the process before reduction, e.g.,
\Omega\vdash P::\Gamma,x:A
.
Then, by the IH,
(\Omega\vdash P::\Gamma,x:A)\cong(\Omega\vdash Q::\Gamma^{\prime},x:A^{\prime})
.
The thesis then follows from an application of Rules
[
unf-par
]
and
[
unf-res
]
.

The case of Rule
[
red-cong
]
follows from the IH and subject congruence (
Theorem

7
).
To be precise, the rule says that (i)
P\equiv P^{\prime}
, (ii)
P^{\prime}\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q^{\prime}
, and (iii)
Q^{\prime}\equiv Q
imply
P\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q
.
By
Theorem

7
(subject congruence) on assumptions (i) and (iii), we have
(\Omega\vdash P::\Gamma)\cong(\Omega\vdash P^{\prime}::\Gamma^{\prime})
and
(\Omega^{\prime}\vdash Q^{\prime}::\Delta^{\prime})\cong(\Omega^{\prime}\vdash Q::\Delta)
.
Also, by the IH on assumption (ii),
(\Omega\vdash P^{\prime}::\Gamma^{\prime})\cong(\Omega^{\prime}\vdash Q^{\prime}::\Delta^{\prime})
where
\Omega=\Omega^{\prime}
.
Then, by
5
(transitivity of
\cong
),
(\Omega\vdash P::\Gamma)\cong(\Omega\vdash Q::\Delta)
, proving the thesis.

Key cases are Rules
[
red-send-recv
]
,
[
red-sel-bra
]
, and
[
red-fwd
]
.

Figure

7
shows the representative instance of Rule
[
red-send-recv
]
, and an example where Rules
[
red-res
]
,
[
red-par
]
, and
[
red-cong
]
are used.

Protocol fidelity ensures that processes respect their intended (session) protocols.
Communication safety ensures the absence of communication errors and mismatches in processes.
Correct typability gives a static guarantee that a process conforms to its ascribed session protocols; type preservation gives a dynamic guarantee.
Because session types describe the intended protocols and error-free exchanges, type preservation entails both protocol fidelity and communication safety.
For a detailed account, we refer the curious reader to the early work by Honda
et al.

[
HVK98
]
, which defines error processes
and shows by contradiction that well-typed processes do not reduce to an error.

In what follows, we consider a process to be deadlocked if it is not the inactive process and cannot reduce.
Our deadlock-freedom result for
APCP
adapts that for
PCP

[
DG18
]
.
The equivalent of our Rule [
typ-res
] in
PCP
is Rule [
cycle
].
Deadlock freedom for
PCP
involves three steps to eliminate applications of Rule [
cycle
]:

(1)

First, [
cycle
] elimination states that we can remove all applications of [
cycle
] in a typing derivation without affecting the derivation’s assumptions and conclusion.

(2)

Only the removal of
top-level
applications of [
cycle
] captures the intended process semantics; the removal of other applications of [
cycle
] corresponds to reductions behind prefixes, which is not allowed
[
Wad12
,
DG18
]
.
Therefore, the second step is
top-level deadlock freedom
(referred to here as
progress
), which states that a process with a top-level application of [
cycle
] reduces until there are no top-level applications of [
cycle
] left.
This step requires commuting conversions: a process with a top-level application of [
cycle
] may not have reductions ready, so commuting conversions are used to remove top-level applications of [
cycle
] by blocking them with prefixes.

(3)

Third, deadlock freedom follows for processes typable under empty contexts.

Here, we adapt and address [
typ-res
] elimination and progress in one proof.

As mentioned before, binding
APCP
’s asynchronous sends and selections to continuations involves additional, low-level uses of [
typ-res
], which we cannot eliminate through process reduction.
Therefore, we establish progress for
live processes
(
Theorem

9
).
A process is live if it is equivalent to a restriction on
active names
used for unguarded prefixes.
This way, e.g., in
x[y,z]
the name
x
is active, but
y
and
z
are not.
We additionally need a notion of
evaluation context
, under which reducible forwarders may occur.

{defi}

[Active Names]

The
set of active names
of
P
, denoted
\an(P)
, contains the (free) names that are used for non-blocked prefixed:

\displaystyle\an(x[y,z])

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\{x\}

\displaystyle\an(x(y,z);P)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\{x\}

\displaystyle\an(\bm{0})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\emptyset

\displaystyle\an(x[z]\triangleleft\ell)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\{x\}

\displaystyle\an(x(z)\triangleright\{i:P_{i}\}_{i\in I})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\{x\}

\displaystyle\an([x\leftrightarrow y])

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\emptyset

\displaystyle\an(P\mathbin{|}Q)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\an(P)\cup\an(Q)

\displaystyle\an(\mu X(\tilde{x});P)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\an(P)

\displaystyle\an((\bm{\nu}xy)P)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\an(P)\setminus\{x,y\}

\displaystyle\an(X\langle\tilde{x}\rangle)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\emptyset

{defi}

[Evaluation Context]

Evaluation contexts (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E}}
) are defined by the following grammar:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E}}::={\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}[\cdot]}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E}}\mathbin{|}P\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}(\bm{\nu}xy){\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}\mu X(\tilde{x});{\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E}}

We write
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}[P\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}]
to denote the process obtained by replacing the hole
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}[\cdot]}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E}}
by
P
.

{defi}

[Live Process]

A process
P
is
live
, denoted
\live(P)
, if

(1)

there are names
x,y
and process
P^{\prime}
such that
P\equiv(\bm{\nu}xy)P^{\prime}
with
x,y\in\an(P^{\prime})
, or

(2)

there are names
x,y,z
and process
P^{\prime}
such that
P\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\big[(\bm{\nu}yz)([x\leftrightarrow y]\mathbin{|}P^{\prime})\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\big]
and
z\neq x
(i.e., the forwarder is independent).

We additionally need to account for recursion: as recursive definitions do not directly entail reductions, we must fully unfold them before eliminating applications of [
typ-res
]:

Lemma 3.7

(Unfolding)
.

If
\Omega\vdash P::\Gamma
, then there is a process
P^{\star}
such that
P^{\star}\equiv P
and
P^{\star}
is not of the form
\mu X(\tilde{z});Q
.

Proof 3.8
.

By induction on the number
n
of consecutive recursive definitions prefixing
P
, such that
P
is of the form
\mu X_{1}(\tilde{z});\ldots;\mu X_{n}(\tilde{z});Q
.
If
n=0
, the thesis follows immediately, as
P\equiv P
.
Otherwise,
n\geq 1
.
Then there are
X,Q
such that
P=\mu X(\tilde{z});Q
, where
Q
starts with
n-1
consecutive recursive definitions.
Let
R\mathrel{\triangleq}Q\big\{\big(\mu X(\tilde{y});Q\{\tilde{y}/\tilde{z}\}\big)/X\langle\tilde{y}\rangle\big\}
.
Clearly,
R\equiv P
.
Then, because
R
starts with
n-1
consecutive recursive definitions, the thesis follows by appealing to the IH.

Dardha and Gay’s progress result concerns a sequence of reduction steps that reaches a process that is not live anymore
[
DG18
]
.
In our case, progress concerns a single reduction step only, because recursive processes might stay live across reductions forever.
Moreover, because of our definition of liveness, we do not need commuting conversions for this step.

Theorem 3.8

(Progress)
.

If
\emptyset\vdash P::\Gamma
and
\live(P)
, then there is a process
Q
such that
P\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q
.

Proof 3.9
.

We distinguish the two cases of
\live(P)
:
P
contains a restriction on a pair of active names, or
P
contains a restriction on a forwarded name under a reduction context.
In both cases, we first unfold any recursive definitions preceding the involved prefixes/forwarders, resulting in
P^{\star}\equiv P
.
By subject congruence (
Theorem

7
), there exists
\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
\emptyset\vdash P^{\star}::\Gamma^{\prime}
and
(\vdash P^{\star}::\Gamma^{\prime})\mathrel{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{\scalebox{1}[.8]{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\lesssim$}}}}\Gamma
.
By
Section

3.3
, the only difference between
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\Gamma
is the unfolding of recursive types.

(1)

P^{\star}
contains a restriction on a pair of active names.
That is,
P^{\star}\equiv(\bm{\nu}xy){P^{\star}}^{\prime}
and
x,y\in\an({P^{\star}}^{\prime})
.
The rest of the analysis depends on how
x
and
y
occur as active names in
{P^{\star}}^{\prime}
.
As a representative case, we consider that
x
occurs as the subject of a send.
By inversion of typing, then
y
occurs as the subject of a receive.
Hence, we have
P^{\star}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\big[(\bm{\nu}xy)(x[a,b]\mathbin{|}y(v,z);{P^{\star}}^{\prime\prime})\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\big]
.
Let
Q\mathrel{\triangleq}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}[{P^{\star}}^{\prime\prime}\{a/v,b/z\}\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}]
.
Then, by Rule
[
red-send-recv
]
,
P^{\star}\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q
.
Hence, by Rule
[
red-cong
]
,
P\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q
, proving the thesis.

(2)

P^{\star}
contains a restriction on a forwarded name under a reduction context.
That is,
P^{\star}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\big[(\bm{\nu}yz)([x\leftrightarrow y]\mathbin{|}{P^{\star}}^{\prime})\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\big]
where
z\neq x
.
Let
Q={\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}[{P^{\star}}^{\prime}\{x/z\}\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}]
.
Then, by Rule
[
red-fwd
]
,
P^{\star}\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q
.
Hence, by Rule
[
red-cong
]
,
P\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q
, proving the thesis.

Our deadlock-freedom result concerns processes typable under empty contexts (as in, e.g., Caires and Pfenning
[
CP10
]
and Dardha and Gay
[
DG18
]
).
We first need a lemma which ensures that non-live processes typable under empty contexts do not contain prefixes (sends/receives/selections/branches) or
independent
forwarders (whose endpoints are not bound together using restriction).
This lemma is in fact the crux of our deadlock-freedom result, as it relies on the priority checks induced by our typing system:

Lemma 3.10
.

If
\emptyset\vdash P::\emptyset
and
P
is not live, then
P
contains no prefixes or independent forwarders.

Proof 3.11
.

W.l.o.g., assume all recursion in
P
is unfolded.
Take
P^{\star}\equiv P
such that
P^{\star}=(\bm{\nu}x_{i}x^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}{\mathchoice{\textstyle}{}{}{}\prod}_{j\in J}P_{j}
where, for every
j\in J
,
P_{j}
is a prefix or forwarder.
That is, every
P_{j}
is a
thread
which cannot be broken down further into parallel components or restrictions.

By abuse of notation, we write
\pr(P_{j})
to denote the priority of the type of the subject of the prefix of
P_{j}
.
Also, we write
\pr(x)
to denote the priority of the type of
x
.

Towards a contradiction, assume that there is at least one prefix or independent forwarder in
P
.
We apply induction on the size of
J
.

•

In the base case,
J=\emptyset
.
Then there cannot be any prefixes or independent forwarders in
P
: a contradiction.

•

In the inductive case,
J=J^{\prime}\cup\{j\}
.
W.l.o.g., assume that, for every
j^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
,
\pr(P_{j})\leq\pr(P_{j^{\prime}})
(i.e., pick
P_{j}
as one of the prefixes/forwarders with the least priority of all threads).
By assumption,
P_{j}
denotes a prefix or forwarder on some endpoint
x_{i}
.
By well typedness,
x_{i}
is connected through restriction to
x^{\prime}_{i}
.
By Rule
[
typ-res
]
,
\pr(x_{i})=\pr(x^{\prime}_{i})
.
Further analysis depends on
P_{j}
: a receive (or, analogously, a branch), a send (or, analogously, a selection), or a forwarder.

–

Suppose
P_{j}=x_{i}(y,z);P^{\prime}_{j}
.
By Rule
[
typ-recv
]
,
x^{\prime}_{i}\notin\fn(P^{\prime}_{j})
, because the rule requires
\pr(x_{i})<\pr(x^{\prime}_{i})
: otherwise, this would contradict the fact that
\pr(x_{i})=\pr(x^{\prime}_{i})
.
Hence, there exists
j^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
such that
x^{\prime}_{i}\in\fn(P_{j^{\prime}})
.

If
J^{\prime}=\emptyset
, the contradiction is immediate.
Otherwise, the analysis depends on the prefix of
P_{j^{\prime}}
.
Since
P
is not live, this cannot be a send with subject
x^{\prime}_{i}
or an independent forwarder on
x^{\prime}_{i}
.
The prefix can also not be a dependent forwarder, for
x_{i}
already appears in
P_{j}
.
This leaves us with two possibilities: a receive (or, analogously, a branch) on another endpoint, or a send (or, analogously, a selection) with object
x^{\prime}_{i}
.

*

If
P_{j^{\prime}}=x_{k}(v,w);P^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}
where
x^{\prime}_{i}\in\fn(P^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}})
, then, by Rule
[
typ-recv
]
,
\pr(x_{k})<\pr(x^{\prime}_{i})
.
Hence,
\pr(P_{j^{\prime}})=\pr(x_{k})<\pr(x^{\prime}_{i})=\pr(x_{i})=\pr(P_{j})
: this contradicts the assumption that
\pr(P_{j})\leq\pr(P_{j^{\prime}})
.

*

If
P_{j^{\prime}}=x[a,b]
where
x^{\prime}_{i}\in\{a,b\}
, w.l.o.g., assume
x^{\prime}_{i}=a
.
Then, by Rule
[
typ-send
]
,
\pr(x_{k})<\pr(x^{\prime}_{i})
.
The contradiction follows as in the previous case.

–

Suppose
P_{j}=x_{i}[a,b]
.
By Rule
[
typ-send
]
,
x^{\prime}_{i}\notin\{a,b\}
, because the rule requires
\pr(x_{i})<\pr(x^{\prime}_{i})
: otherwise, this would contradict the fact that
\pr(x_{i})=\pr(x^{\prime}_{i})
.
Hence, there exists
j^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
such that
x^{\prime}_{i}\in\fn(P_{j^{\prime}})
.
The contradiction follows as in the previous case.

–

Suppose
P_{j}=[x_{i}\leftrightarrow x_{k}]
.
By well typedness,
x_{i}
is connected through restriction to
x^{\prime}_{i}
.
Since
P
is not live, it must be that
x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{k}
:
P_{j}
is not an independent forwarder.
Then there must be a
j^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
where
P_{j^{\prime}}
fulfills the assumption that
P
contains at least one prefix or independent forwarder.
The contradiction then follows from the IH.

We now state our deadlock-freedom result:

Theorem 3.11

(Deadlock Freedom)
.

If
\emptyset\vdash P::\emptyset
, then either
P\equiv\bm{0}
or
P\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q\mkern 2.0mu
for some
Q
.

Proof 3.12
.

The analysis depends on whether
P
is live or not.

•

If
P
is not live, then, by
Lemma

3.10
, it does not contain any prefixes or independent forwarders.
Any recursive definitions in
P
are thus of the form
\mu X_{1}();\ldots;\mu X_{n}();\bm{0}
: contractiveness requires recursive calls to be prefixed by receives/branches or bound to parallel sends/selections/forwarders, of which there are none.
Hence, we can use structural congruence to rewrite each recursive definition in
P
to
\bm{0}
by unfolding, yielding
P^{\prime}\equiv P
.
Any dependent forwarders in
P
are of the form
(\bm{\nu}xy)[x\leftrightarrow y]
, and can be rewritten to
\bm{0}
using Rule
[
cong-res-fwd
]
(cf.
Figure

2
).
The remaining derivation of
P^{\prime}
only contains applications of Rule
[
typ-inact
]
,
[
typ-par
]
,
[
typ-end
]
, or
[
typ-res
]
on closed names.
It follows straightforwardly that
P\equiv P^{\prime}\equiv\bm{0}
.

•

If
P
is live, by
Theorem

9
there is
Q
s.t.
P\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q
.

3.4.
Reactivity

Progress (
Theorem

9
) is an important liveness property, as it defines precisely the conditions under which processes can reduce (namely, liveness in
Section

3.3
).
As such, progress plays a key role in deadlock freedom (
Theorem

10
).
However, in the presence of recursion, the strength of progress is limited: even if the majority of subprocesses is stuck, progress holds if at least one subprocess is live.
Nonetheless,
APCP
’s type system allows us to prove a stronger result:
reactivity
, which essentially states that infinite recursion in
APCP
cannot block sessions from progressing.

Processes typable under empty contexts are not only deadlock free, they are
reactive
, in the following sense: for each name in the process, we can eventually observe a reduction involving that name.
To formalize this property, we define
labeled reductions
, which expose details about synchronizations:

{defi}

[Labeled Reductions]

Consider the labels

\displaystyle\alpha::=[x\leftrightarrow y]\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}x\rangle y:a\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}x\rangle y:\ell\qquad\qquad\text{(forwarding, send/receive, selection/branching)}

where each label has subjects
x
and
y
.
The
labeled reduction

P\mathrel{\xrightharpoondown{\alpha}}Q
is defined by the following rules:

\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{lred-send-recv}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 92.73991pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to193.47983pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$(\bm{\nu}xy)(x[a,b]\mathbin{|}y(v,z);P)\mathrel{\xrightharpoondown{x\rangle y:a}}P\{a/v,b/z\}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{lred-sel-bra}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 91.79851pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$j\in I$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to213.69016pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$(\bm{\nu}xy)(x[b]\triangleleft j\mathbin{|}y(z)\triangleright\{i:P_{i}\}_{i\in I})\mathrel{\xrightharpoondown{x\rangle y:j}}P_{j}\{b/z\}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{lred-fwd}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 69.23897pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to146.47795pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$(\bm{\nu}yz)([x\leftrightarrow y]\mathbin{|}P)\mathrel{\xrightharpoondown{[x\leftrightarrow y]}}P\{x/z\}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{lred-cong}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$P\equiv P^{\prime}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$P^{\prime}\mathrel{\xrightharpoondown{\alpha}}Q^{\prime}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$Q^{\prime}\equiv Q$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to140.36101pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 55.72383pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$P\mathrel{\xrightharpoondown{\alpha}}Q$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{lred-res}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 23.69449pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$P\mathrel{\xrightharpoondown{\alpha}}Q$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to76.30232pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$(\bm{\nu}xy)P\mathrel{\xrightharpoondown{\alpha}}(\bm{\nu}xy)Q$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{lred-par}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 10.44794pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$P\mathrel{\xrightharpoondown{\alpha}}Q$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to49.80922pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$P\mathbin{|}R\mathrel{\xrightharpoondown{\alpha}}Q\mathbin{|}R$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\end{array}

Proposition 3.13
.

For any
P
and
P^{\prime}
,
P\mathbin{\rightarrow}P^{\prime}
if and only if there exists a label
\alpha
such that
P\mathrel{\xrightharpoondown{\alpha}}P^{\prime}
.

Proof 3.14
.

Immediate by definition, because each reduction in
Figure

2
(bottom) corresponds to a labeled reduction, and vice versa.

Our reactivity result states that processes typable under empty contexts have at least one finite reduction sequence (denoted
\mathbin{\rightarrow}^{\mkern-3.0mu\star}
) that enables a labeled reduction involving a
pending
name—a name that occurs as the subject of a prefix and is not bound by an input (see below).
Clearly, the typed process may have other reduction sequences, not necessarily finite.

{defi}

[Pending Names]

Given a process
P
, we define the set of
pending names
of
P
, denoted
\pn(P)
, as follows:

\displaystyle\pn(x[y,z])

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\{x\}

\displaystyle\pn(x(y,z);P)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\{x\}\cup(\pn(P)\setminus\{y,z\})

\displaystyle\pn(x[z]\triangleleft\ell)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\{x\}

\displaystyle\pn(x(z)\triangleright\{i:P_{i}\}_{i\in I})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\{x\}\cup({\mathchoice{\textstyle}{}{}{}\bigcup}_{i\in I}\pn(P_{i})\setminus\{z\})

\displaystyle\pn(P\mathbin{|}Q)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\pn(P)\cup\pn(Q)

\displaystyle\pn(\mu X(\tilde{x});P)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\pn(P)

\displaystyle\pn(\bm{0})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\emptyset

\displaystyle\pn((\bm{\nu}xy)P)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\pn(P)

\displaystyle\pn(X\langle\tilde{x}\rangle)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\emptyset

\displaystyle\pn([x\leftrightarrow y])

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\{x,y\}

Note that the proof of reactivity below does not rely on deadlock freedom: suppose we are observing a blocked pending prefix in a process with a parallel recursive definition; deadlock freedom ensures a reduction from the recursive definition which would not unblock the pending prefix we are observing.
Instead, the proof relies on a priority analysis (similar to the one in the proof of
Lemma

3.10
) to unblock pending prefixes.

Theorem 3.14

(Reactivity)
.

Suppose given a process
\emptyset\vdash P::\emptyset
.
Then, for every
x\in\pn(P)
there exists a process
P^{\prime}
such that
P\mathbin{\rightarrow}^{\mkern-3.0mu\star}P^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}\mathrel{\xrightharpoondown{\alpha}}Q
, for some process
Q
and label
\alpha
with subject
x
.

Proof 3.15
.

Take any
x\in\pn(P)
.
Because
P
is typable under empty contexts,
x
is bound to some
y\in\pn(P)
by restriction.
By typing, in
P
there is exactly one prefix on
x
and one prefix on
y
(they may also occur in forwarder processes).
Following the restrictions on priorities in the typing of
x
and
y
in
P
, the prefixes on
x
and
y
cannot occur sequentially in
P
(cf. the proof of
Lemma

3.10
for details on this reasoning).
By typability, the prefix on
y
is dual to the prefix on
x
.

We apply induction on the number of receives, branches, and recursive definitions in
P
blocking the prefixes on
x
and
y
, denoted
n
and
m
, respectively.
Because
P
is typable under empty contexts, the blocking receives and branches that are on names in
\pn(P)
also have to be bound to pending names by restriction.
The prefixes on these connected names may also be prefixed by receives, branches, and recursive definitions, so we may need to unblock those prefixes as well.
Since there can only be a finite number of names in any given process, we also apply induction on the number of prefixes blocking these connected prefixes.

•

If
n=0
and
m=0
, then the prefixes on
x
and
y
occur at the top level; because they do not occur sequentially, the synchronization between
x
and
y
can take place immediately.
Hence,
P\mathrel{\xrightharpoondown{\alpha}}Q
where
x
and
y
are the subjects of
\alpha
.
This proves the thesis, with
P^{\prime}=P
.

•

If
n>0
or
m>0
, the analysis depends on the foremost prefixes blocking the prefixes on
x
and
y
.

If the either of these blocking prefixes is a recursive definition (
\mu X(\tilde{y})
), we unfold the recursion.
Because a corresponding recursive call (
X\langle\tilde{z}\rangle
) cannot occur as a prefix, the effect of unfolding either (i) triggers prefixes that occur in parallel to those on
x
and
y
, or (ii) the prefixes on
x
or
y
precede the punfolded recursive call.
In either case, the number of prefixes decreases, and the thesis follows from the IH.

Otherwise, if neither foremost prefix is a recursive definition, then the foremost prefixes must be on names in
\pn(P)
.
Consider the prefix that is typable with the least priority.
W.l.o.g. assume that this is the foremost prefix of
x
.
Suppose this prefix is on some name
w
connected to another name
z\in\pn(P)
by restriction.
By typability, the priority of
w
is less than that of
x
and all of the prefixes in between.
This means that the number of prefixes blocking the prefix on
z
strictly decreases.
Hence, by the IH,
P\mathbin{\rightarrow}^{\mkern-3.0mu\star}P^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\xrightharpoondown{\alpha^{\prime}}}Q^{\prime}
in a finite number of steps, where
w
and
z
are the subjects of
\alpha^{\prime}
.
The synchronization between
w
and
z
can be performed, and
n
decreases.
By type preservation (
8
),
\emptyset\vdash Q^{\prime}::\emptyset
.
The thesis then follows from the IH:
P\mathbin{\rightarrow}^{\mkern-3.0mu\star}P^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\xrightharpoondown{\alpha^{\prime}}}Q^{\prime}\mathbin{\rightarrow}^{\mkern-3.0mu\star}P^{\prime}\mathrel{\xrightharpoondown{\alpha}}Q
in finite steps, where
x
and
y
are the subjects of
\alpha
.

Note that deadlock freedom (
Theorem

10
) can be derived directly from reactivity: liveness implies the existence of pending names, so reduction is guaranteed by
Theorem

11
.
In the following, deadlock freedom is strong enough, but in
[
HP22b
]
where we apply
APCP
for the analysis of distributed implementations of multiparty session types we do rely on the stronger reactivity.

3.5.
Typing Milner’s Cyclic Scheduler

Figure 8.
Typing derivation of the leader scheduler
A_{1}
of Milner’s cyclic scheduler (processes omitted).

Here we show that our specification of Milner’s cyclic scheduler from
Section

2.1
is typable in
APCP
, and thus deadlock free (cf.
Theorem

10
).
Let us recall the process definitions of the leader and followers, omitting braces ‘
\{\ldots\}
’ for branches with one option:

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\mu X({\color[rgb]{0.1836,0.3125,0.0703}a_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}c_{n}},{\color[rgb]{0.1602,0.2305,0.418}d_{1}});\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.1602,0.2305,0.418}d_{1}}}\triangleleft\mathsf{start}\cdot\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.1836,0.3125,0.0703}a_{1}}}\triangleleft\mathsf{start}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1836,0.3125,0.0703}a_{1}}\triangleright\mathsf{ack};\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.1602,0.2305,0.418}d_{1}}}\triangleleft\mathsf{next}\cdot\\

\displaystyle A_{i+1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\mu X({\color[rgb]{0.1836,0.3125,0.0703}a_{i+1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}c_{i}},{\color[rgb]{0.1602,0.2305,0.418}d_{i+1}});\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}c_{i}}\triangleright\mathsf{start};\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.1836,0.3125,0.0703}a_{i+1}}}\triangleleft\mathsf{start}\cdot\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.1602,0.2305,0.418}d_{i+1}}}\triangleleft\mathsf{start}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1836,0.3125,0.0703}a_{i+1}}\triangleright\mathsf{ack};\\
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}c_{i}}\triangleright\mathsf{next};\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.1602,0.2305,0.418}d_{i+1}}}\triangleleft\mathsf{next}\cdot X\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1836,0.3125,0.0703}a_{i+1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}c_{i}},{\color[rgb]{0.1602,0.2305,0.418}d_{i+1}}\rangle\end{array}\qquad\forall 1\leq i<n

8
gives the typing derivation of
A_{1}
, omitting processes from judgments, with the following priority requirements:

\displaystyle t_{1}

\displaystyle>{\mathchoice{\textstyle}{}{}{}\max}(\circ_{1},\kappa_{1},\pi_{n},\rho_{n},\pi_{1},\rho_{1})

(3)

\displaystyle\pi_{1}

\displaystyle<\rho_{1}

(4)

\displaystyle\circ_{1}

\displaystyle<\kappa_{1}

\displaystyle<\pi_{n},\rho_{1}

\displaystyle<\circ_{1}+t_{1},\pi_{1}+t_{1}

\displaystyle\rho_{n}

\displaystyle<\circ_{1}+t_{1},\pi_{1}+t_{1}

(9)

Each process
A_{i+1}
for
0\leq i<n
—thus including the leader—is typable as follows, assuming
c_{i}
is
c_{n}
for
i=0
:

\displaystyle\emptyset

\displaystyle\vdash A_{i+1}::{\color[rgb]{0.1836,0.3125,0.0703}a_{i+1}}:\mu X.{\oplus}^{\circ_{i+1}}\{\mathsf{start}:\&^{\kappa_{i+1}}\{\mathsf{ack}:X\}\},\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}c_{i}}:\mu X.\&^{\pi_{i}}\{\mathsf{start}:\&^{\rho_{i}}\{\mathsf{next}:X\}\},\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1602,0.2305,0.418}d_{i+1}}:\mu X.{\oplus}^{\pi_{i+1}}\{\mathsf{start}:{\oplus}^{\rho_{i+1}}\{\mathsf{next}:\hskip-2.0ptX\}\}\end{array}

Note how, for each
1\leq i\leq n
, the types for
c_{i}
and
d_{i}
are duals and are thus assigned equal priorities.

The priority requirements in the typing derivation of each
A_{i}
are satisfiable.
The derivations of these processes have the following constraints:

•

For
A_{1}
we require the inequalities listed above;

•

For each
1\leq i<n
, for
A_{i+1}
we require
\pi_{i}<\circ_{i+1},\pi_{i+1}
,
\circ_{i+1}<\kappa_{i+1}
,
\pi_{i+1}<\rho_{i+1}
,
\kappa_{i+1}<\rho_{i},\rho_{i+1}
,
\rho_{i}<\circ_{i+1}+t_{i+1},\rho_{i+1}
, and
\rho_{i+1}<\pi_{i+1}+t_{i+1}
.

We can satisfy these requirements by assigning
\pi_{i}\mathrel{\triangleq}i
,
\circ_{i}\mathrel{\triangleq}i+1
,
\kappa_{i}\mathrel{\triangleq}i+2
, and
\rho_{i}\mathrel{\triangleq}i+4
for each
1\leq i\leq n
, except with
\pi_{n}\mathrel{\triangleq}n+3
(to satisfy (
6
) for
1\leq n<4
).
For the application of [
typ-rec
], each derivation also requires a common lifter
t_{i+1}>{\mathchoice{\textstyle}{}{}{}\max}(\circ_{i+1},\kappa_{i+1},\pi_{i},\rho_{i},\pi_{i+1},\rho_{i+1})
for
0\leq i<n
.
The priority requirements involving
t_{i+1}
always require the priority lifted by
t_{i+1}
to be higher than the priority not lifted by
t_{i+1}
, so the common lifter requirement easily satisfies these requirements.

Recall from
Section

2.1
:

Sched_{n}\mathrel{\triangleq}(\bm{\nu}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}c_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.1602,0.2305,0.418}d_{1}})\ldots(\bm{\nu}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}c_{n}}{\color[rgb]{0.1602,0.2305,0.418}d_{n}})\big((\bm{\nu}{\color[rgb]{0.1836,0.3125,0.0703}a_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.7578,0.3867,0.8945}b_{1}})(A_{1}\mathbin{|}P_{1})\mathbin{|}\ldots\mathbin{|}(\bm{\nu}{\color[rgb]{0.1836,0.3125,0.0703}a_{n}}{\color[rgb]{0.7578,0.3867,0.8945}b_{n}})(A_{n}\mathbin{|}P_{n})\big)

Assuming given workers

\emptyset\vdash P_{i}::{\color[rgb]{0.7578,0.3867,0.8945}b_{i}}:\mu X.\&^{\circ_{i}}\{\mathsf{start}:{\oplus}^{\kappa_{i}}\{\mathsf{ack}:X\}\}

for each
1\leq i\leq n
, we have
\emptyset\vdash Sched_{n}::\emptyset
.
Hence, it follows from
Theorem

10
that
Sched_{n}
is deadlock free for each
n\geq 1
.

3.6.
Extensions: Explicit Session Closing and Replicated Servers

\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{typ-close}]}}}}\\

\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{red-close-wait}]}}}}\\

\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 110.2222pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to228.4444pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$(\bm{\nu}xy)({?}x[a]\mathbin{|}{!}y(v);P\mathbin{|}Q)\mathbin{\rightarrow}P\{a/v\}\mathbin{|}(\bm{\nu}xy)({!}y(v);P\mathbin{|}Q)$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\end{array}

Figure 9.
Typing rules (top) and reductions (bottom) for explicit closing and replicated servers.

As already mentioned, our presentation of
APCP
does not include explicit closing and replicated servers.
Here we briefly discuss what
APCP
would look like if we were to include these constructs.

Explicit closing is useful in programming to be sure that all resources are cleaned up correctly.
There are several ways of integrating explicit closing in a calculus like
APCP
.
Following, e.g.,
[
CP10
,
Wad12
]
, here we achieve explicit closing by adding closes (empty sends)
x[]
and waits (empty receives)
x();P
to the syntax in
Figure

2
(top).
We also add the Rule [
red-close-wait
] to
Figure

2
(bottom).
At the level of types, we replace the conflated type
\bullet
with
\bm{1}^{\circ}
and
\bot^{\circ}
, associated to closes and waits, respectively.
Note that we do need priority annotations on types for closed names now, because wait is blocking and thus requires priority checks.
In the type system in
Figure

4
(top), we replace Rule [
typ-end
] with Rules [
type-close
] and [
typ-wait
] in
Figure

9
(top).

For replicated servers, we add (asynchronous) client requests
{?}x[y]
and servers
{!}x(y);P
, with types
{?}^{\circ}A
and
{!}^{\circ}A
, respectively.
We include syntactic sugar for binding client requests to continuations as in
Section

3.1
:
{?}\overline{x}[y]\cdot P\mathrel{\triangleq}(\bm{\nu}ya)({?}x[a]\mathbin{|}P)
.
Rule [
red-cli-srv
] is in
Figure

9
(bottom): it connects a client and a server and forks a copy of the server.
Also, we add a structural congruence rule to clean up unused servers:
(\bm{\nu}xz)({!}x(y);P)\equiv\bm{0}
.
In the type system, we add Rules [
typ-cli
], [
typ-srv
], [
typ-weaken
], and [
typ-contract
] in
Figure

9
(top); the former two are for typing client requests and servers, respectively, and the latter two are for connecting to a server without requests and for multiple requests, respectively.
In Rule [
typ-srv
], notation
{?}\Gamma
means that every type in
\Gamma
is of the form
{?}^{\circ}A
.

Figure

9
(top) also includes a derivable Rule [
typ-cli
\ast
] which types the syntactic sugar for bound client requests.

4.
An Intermezzo: From
APCP
to
LAST

As attested by its expressivity and
meta-theoretical results (
8
and
10
),
APCP
provides a convenient framework for analyzing asynchronous message passing between cyclically connected processes.
In particular,
APCP
provides a firm basis for designing languages with session-typed concurrency, asynchronous communication, and cyclic structures.
Ideally, we would like to faithfully compile any such language into
APCP
, in order to transfer its correctness guarantees.

As discussed in the Introduction, we look for answers in the realm of functional programming, in the form of variants of the
\lambda
-calculus with session-typed, message-passing concurrency.
In this context, Gay and Vasconcelos’s
LAST

[
GV10
]
appears nicely positioned:
LAST
is a call-by-value language in which programs consist of threads that are cyclically connected on channels that provide asynchronous message passing (through buffers).
The calculus
LAST
has forked several variants that connect message-passing processes with message-passing functions, as we set out to do here.
Most notably, Walder
[
Wad12
,
Wad14
]
introduced
GV
, a variant of
LAST
with synchronous communication and non-cyclic thread connections, and gave a translation into his
CP
(the synchronous ancestor of
APCP
, without cyclic connections).
Subsequently, in the same spirit, Kokke and Dardha
[
KD21a
]
presented
PGV
, a variant of
GV
with cyclic connections, and a translation from Dardha and Gay’s
PCP
(the synchronous ancestor of
APCP

[
DG18
]
).

Hence,
LAST
is a natural choice for a core programming language that can be studied via a translation into
APCP
.
Notice that both
GV
and
PGV
enjoy deadlock freedom by typing, whereas
LAST
does not.
This strengthens our motivation for designing a translation into
APCP
, as transferring the deadlock-freedom property from
APCP
to
LAST
would address a significant gap.
To our knowledge, such a
translational
approach to ensuring deadlock freedom for
LAST
programs has not been achieved before.

In this section we gently recall
LAST
and gradually introduce the key ingredients for its translation into
APCP
.
For presentational purposes, we find it useful to present a variant of
LAST
that is more convenient towards a translation, denoted
LAST
\star
(
Section

4.1
).
The type system for
LAST
\star
, described in
Section

4.2
, closely follows the one for
LAST
in
[
GV10
]
.
Then, in
Section

4.3
, we discuss the potential design of a translation from
LAST
\star
into
APCP
.
We purposefully use the word “potential”: we seek a translation that is
faithful
, i.e., that preserves and reflects behaviors in a precise sense.
Given this focus, we shall argue that the call-by-value semantics of
LAST
\star
is not well suited for inducing a faithful translation.
As such, this section serves as motivation for introducing
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
, a variant of
LAST
based on a call-by-name semantics with constructs for session closing and explicit substitutions, which enjoys a faithful translation into
APCP
and admits the translational approach to the deadlock-freedom property (
Section

5
).

4.1.
The Syntax and Semantics of
LAST
\star

In
LAST
\star
, programs consist of two layers: while
terms
(
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M,N,\ldots}
) define the behavior of threads,
configurations
(
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C,D,\ldots}
) are obtained by composing threads in parallel, so as to enable their interaction by exchanging messages on buffered channels.
Before detailing these syntactic elements (and their semantics), we point out the differences between
LAST
and
LAST
\star
.
While in
LAST
endpoints are created by synchronization on shared names,
LAST
\star
features a dedicated construct for endpoint creation (denoted
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{new}}}
).
Also, thread creation in
LAST
\star
involves an explicit continuation, not present in
LAST
.
Moreover, in
LAST
buffers run next to threads, whereas they are integrated in endpoint restrictions in
LAST
\star
.
Finally, for simplicity,
LAST
\star
accounts for linear sessions only.

Terms

Terms (
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M},{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}N},\ldots
), values (
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}v}
), and reduction contexts (
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{R}}
):

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M},{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}N}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{\kern.5ex}lr@{\kern 2ex}l@{\kern.5ex}lr@{}}{}::=\hfil\kern 2.15277pt&{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}&\text{variable}\kern 8.61108pt&\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}\hfil\kern 2.15277pt&{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{new}}}&\text{create new channel}\\
~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}\hfil\kern 2.15277pt&{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}()}&\text{unit value}\kern 8.61108pt&\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}\hfil\kern 2.15277pt&{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{fork}}M;N}&\text{fork ${\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}$ in parallel to ${\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}N}$}\\
~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}\hfil\kern 2.15277pt&{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\lambda x.M}&\text{abstraction}\kern 8.61108pt&\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}\hfil\kern 2.15277pt&{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(M,N)}&\text{pair construction}\\
~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}\hfil\kern 2.15277pt&{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M~N}&\text{application}\kern 8.61108pt&\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}\hfil\kern 2.15277pt&{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{let}}(x,y)=M\mathinner{\mathtt{in}}N}&\text{pair deconstruction}\\
~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}\hfil\kern 2.15277pt&{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{send}}M\,N}&\text{send ${\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}$ along ${\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}N}$}\kern 8.61108pt&\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}\hfil\kern 2.15277pt&{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{select}}\ell\,M}&\text{select label $\ell$ along ${\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}$}\\
~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}\hfil\kern 2.15277pt&{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{recv}}M}&\text{receive along ${\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}$}\kern 8.61108pt&\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}\hfil\kern 2.15277pt&{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{case}}M\mathinner{\mathtt{of}}\{i:N\}_{i\in I}}&\text{offer labels in $I$ along ${\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}$}\end{array}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}v}

\displaystyle::={\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\lambda x.M}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(v,v)}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}()}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{R}}}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{\kern.5ex}l@{}}{}::=\hfil\kern 2.15277pt&{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\cdot]}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{R}}~M}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}v~{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{R}}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}({\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{R}},M)}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(v,{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{R}})}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{let}}(x,y)={\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{R}}\mathinner{\mathtt{in}}M}\\
~~\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}\hfil\kern 2.15277pt&{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{send}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{R}}\,M}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{send}}v\,{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{R}}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{recv}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{R}}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{select}}\ell\,{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{R}}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{case}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{R}}\mathinner{\mathtt{of}}\{i:M\}_{i\in I}}\end{array}

.

Term reduction (
\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{M}}
):

\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{red-lam}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 46.71536pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to101.43073pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(\lambda x.M)~v}\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{M}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M\{v/x\}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{red-pair}]}}}}\\

Figure 10.
The
LAST
\star
term language.

Figure

10
(top) gives the term syntax for
LAST
\star
.
The functional behavior of terms is defined by standard
\lambda
-calculus constructs for variables
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}
, the unit value
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}()}
, abstraction
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\lambda x.M}
and application
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M\ N}
, and pair construction
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(M,N)}
and deconstruction
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{let}}(x,y)=M\mathinner{\mathtt{in}}N}
.
As usual, to improve readability, we often write
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{let}}x=M\mathinner{\mathtt{in}}N}
to denote
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(\lambda x.N)~M}
.

The remaining constructs define the thread and message-passing behavior of terms; their exact semantics will be defined for configurations, so we briefly describe them here.
The construct
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{new}}}
creates a new buffered channel with two endpoints (referred to with variables).
The construct
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{fork}}M;N}
forks a new thread running term
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}
and continues as
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}N}
.
By involving an explicit continuation
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}N}
, this is slightly different than the corresponding construct in
LAST
.
We can think of
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}N}
as the continuation to be run immediately after
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}
is spawned.
This is a mild generalization, as the construct in
LAST
corresponds to the case in which
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}N}={\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}()}
; it will be convenient for the correct translation into
APCP
.

The constructs
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{send}}NM}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{recv}}M}
denote sending and receiving messages along
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}
once it has reduced to a variable referring to a buffer endpoint, respectively; that is, sending entails placing the message
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}N}
at the end of the buffer, and receiving entails taking a message from the start of the buffer (if there).
The constructs
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{select}}\ell\,M}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{case}}M\mathinner{\mathtt{of}}\{i:N_{i}\}_{i\in I}}
denote selecting and offering labels along
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}
once it has reduced to an endpoint variable, respectively; that is, selection entails placing the label
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\ell}
at the end of the buffer, and offering entails taking a label
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}j}\in I
from the start of the buffer (if there) and continuing in the corresponding branch
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}N_{j}}
.

Following
[
GV10
]
, the term reduction semantics of
LAST
\star
employs a call-by-value (CbV) approach;
Figure

10
(center) defines values
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}v}
, i.e., terms that cannot further reduce on their own: variables, abstractions, pairs, and the unit value.

Figure

10
(center) also defines reduction contexts
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{R}}
that define under which positions subterms may reduce.
Finally,
Figure

10
(bottom) defines term reduction (
\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{M}}
).
Rule [
red-lam
] reduces an abstraction applied to a value; the substitution of a value
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}v}
for a (free) variable
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}
is denoted
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\{v/x\}}
, as usual.
Rule [
red-pair
] reduces the pair deconstruction of a pair of values to two substitutions.
Rule [
red-lift
] closes term reduction under reduction contexts.

{exa}

To illustrate the CbV semantics of
LAST
\star
, consider the following term and its behavior:

{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\big(\lambda x.x~(\lambda y.y)\big)~\big((\lambda w.w)~(\lambda z.z)\big)}\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{M}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\big(\lambda x.x~(\lambda y.y)\big)~(\lambda z.z)}\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{M}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(\lambda z.z)~(\lambda y.y)}\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{M}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\lambda y.y}

We will illustrate the message-passing behavior of
LAST
\star
after introducing configurations below.

Configurations

Markers (
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\phi}
), messages (
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}m},{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}n}
), configurations (
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C},{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D},{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}E}
), thread contexts (
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{F}}
), configuration contexts (
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{G}}
):

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\phi}::={}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\mathchoice{\scriptstyle}{\scriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}{}\blacklozenge}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\mathchoice{\scriptstyle}{\scriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}{}\lozenge}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}m},{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}n}::={}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}v}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\ell}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C},{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D},{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}E}::={}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\phi\,M}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(\bm{\nu}x[\vec{m}\rangle y)C}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C\parallel D}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{F}}::={}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{G}}}::={}
