5.2.
Faithfully Translating
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
into
APCP

We now define a typed translation from
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
into
APCP
.
Then, we show that this translation is operationally complete and sound, in the sense of
Section

4.3
.

The Translation: Key Ideas

Before we define the translation, let us discuss its key idea, inspired by Milner’s translation of the
lazy

\lambda
-calculus
[
Mil92
]
.
The most important design decision is how to translate variables.
Variables serve two purposes: (i) as placeholders for future substitutions and (ii) as an access point to buffered channels.
Accordingly, our translation uses variables as sends that enable (i) an explicit substitution or (ii) interactions with a buffer.
Another important design decision is the translation of buffers: the buffer’s types guide the translation to form a sequence of inputs/outputs that are explicitly forwarded (i.e., not using the forwarder process) between the buffer endpoints.

The Translation: Types

Equipped with these ideas about translating variables and buffers, let us give a deeper insight into our translation by taking a look at how it turns functional and session types in
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
into session types in
APCP
.

Before moving on, note that
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
does not guarantee deadlock freedom by typing.
As such, there is no point in annotating types with priorities, as the priority requirements induced by typing translated processes may not always be satisfiable.
We therefore use a special
APCP
typing judgment

~\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\ast}P::\Gamma~

which indicates well typedness of
P
modulo priority annotations and requirements in
\Gamma
(we omit
\Omega
, as
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
is a finite language).
We will recover priorities and deadlock freedom in
Section

5.3
.

Section

5.2
below introduces two forms of type translation: while we use
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}-}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}
to translate the types of terms/configurations, we use
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}-}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis}
to translate the types of variables.
We write
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis}
to denote a component-wise translation, where each assignment
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}
translates to an assignment
x:{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis}
.
As such, our
typed
translation takes, e.g., a typed term
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathtt{M}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}
and returns a typed process

\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}::{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis},z:{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}.

Above,
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
is the process that models the behavior of
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}
on a fresh name
z
(defined hereafter).

Let us now define and explain the two forms of translation of types (that are mutually recursive):

{defi}
[Translation of Types]

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}}\quad\text{(if ${\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\square}$)}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T\times U}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis}}\mathbin{\otimes}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}U}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T\mathbin{\multimap}U}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}U}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\bm{1}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\bullet

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}{!}T.S}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}{\oplus}\{i:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}\&\{i:\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S_{i}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis}}\}_{i\in I}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\mathsf{end}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}\bullet

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}{?}T.S}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis}}\mathbin{\otimes}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\&\{i:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}{\oplus}\{i:\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S_{i}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis}}\}_{i\in I}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\square}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\square}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis}\mathrel{\triangleq}\bullet

Some intuitions follow:

•

Following the intuitions above, the translation
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}-}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis}
(row 1) codifies a variable as a send action that enables further behaviors.

•

The translation
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}-}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}
translates functional types straightforwardly (row 2): pairs send access to their components as variables, abstractions receive their parameter as a variable and then provide the return type, and units have no behavior.

•

The translation of session types (rows 3 and 4) is more interesting: at a first glance they seem to be translated dually.
This is because these are types that belong to buffered channel endpoints.
As such, when a variable is typed
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}{!}T.S}
and we send a term on this variable, the translation sends something along the variable and thus the variable receives.
Additionally, the translation of sends and selections have an extra send: this signifies a handshake between the variable and the buffer, indicating that both are ready to perform the actual send/selection.

{exa}

We illustrate the translation of types by means of an example.
After giving the translation of the type
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}(T\times S)\mathbin{\multimap}{!}T.S}
, we break the resulting
APCP
type down and explain it in terms of the associated behavior of a process translated from a term implementing the type.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}(T\times S)\mathbin{\multimap}{!}T.S}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}

Section

5.2
: the translation for
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}(T\times S)\mathbin{\multimap}{!}T.S}
, in detail.

The Translation: Terms, Configurations, and Buffers

Now we complete the definition of our translation.
We proceed inductively on the typing derivations of terms, configurations, and buffers, obtaining typing derivations of
APCP
processes.
Hence, the definition considers the typing rules in
Figure

13
, including the changes discussed in
Section

5.1
.

•

The translation of a well-typed term, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathtt{M}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
, corresponds to a judgment
{\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\ast}P::\Delta,z:A}
in
APCP
, where
z
is a fresh name that executes the behavior of
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}
, as usual, and the shape of the process
P
, context
\Delta
, and session type
A
will become precise shortly.
Similarly, the translation of a well-typed configuration, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathtt{C}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
, corresponds to the judgment
{\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\ast}P::\Delta,z:A}
, for some
P
,
\Delta
, and
A
.

•

Also,
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathtt{B}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\vec{m}\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{a\rangle b}
, the translation of a well-typed buffer holding messages
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\vec{m}}
, corresponds to the judgment
{\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\ast}P::\Delta,a:A,b:B}
, for some
P
,
\Delta
,
A
, and
B
.
The translated buffer stores the translations of the messages in
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\vec{m}}
and forwards them on the fresh name
b
; the buffer receives new messages on the fresh name
a
.
Note that, if the buffer is empty, the roles of
a
and
b
may be reversed (depending on
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S}
), and the buffer may receive new messages on
b
instead.

Figures

17
,
18
and
19
give the translation of term, configuration and buffer typing rules.
In the figures (and in the rest of the paper), for the sake of readability, we often omit typing information: we write
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
to refer to the typed process associated to
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathtt{M}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
, and similarly for the translations of typed configurations and buffers. The omitted typing information/derivations is made precise by
Theorem

13
, given below.

Instead of explaining the translation separately, the figures include line-by-line explanations.
Recall that we often write ‘
\_
’ to denote a fresh name of type
\bullet
; when sending names denoted ‘
\_
’, we omit binders ‘
(\bm{\nu}\_\_)
’.

Importantly, the translation is
type preserving
using the translations of types introduced in
Section

5.2
.

Section

A.3
contains a detailed proof.

Theorem 5.1

(Type Preservation for the Translation)
.

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathtt{M}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}
, then
\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}::{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis},z:{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}
;

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathtt{C}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}
, then
\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}::{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis},z:{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}
;

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathtt{B}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\vec{m}\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S}
, then
\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\vec{m}\rangle}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{a\rangle b}::{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Gamma}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis},a:\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis}},b:\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llparenthesis{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\overline{S}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrparenthesis}}
.

Design Decisions: Explicit Substitutions and Closing

Having presented our typed translation, we reflect on our design decision to enrich
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
with explicit substitutions and closing.

As we will see next, adopting explicit substitutions leads to a direct operational correctness result.
To see why,
suppose we were to apply substitutions immediately.
As an example, consider function application (
Figure

17
), which entails substituting a variable in the body of the function.
The translation would need to encode the substitution of the translation of this variable.
However, if the variable does not occur under an evaluation context, there is no way in
APCP
to perform such a substitution immediately.
Hence, the translation would still need to encode such
implicit
substitutions in
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}

explicitly
in
APCP
.
This discrepancy would then have to be handled by means of the aforementioned substitution lifting in our operational correspondence results.
Although this is a perfectly viable approach, we prefer to have a more direct operational correspondence, which entails more direct proofs that are not affected by an asymmetric treatment of substitutions.

Our choice for explicit closing of sessions is more pragmatic: it leads to a more compact translation.
Suppose we were to treat closed sessions by silently weakening them.
Consider, e.g., Rule [
red-send
] (
Figure

11
): the
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{send}}}
primitive is replaced by a variable pointing to the buffered channel, even if the session ends after the send.
Hence, the translation would need a separate case for translating variables for closed sessions.
Consequently, the translation would need similar such separate cases anywhere variables/closed sessions may occur.
This would lead to a translation where the key ideas are unnecessarily obfuscated.
We find it then preferable to treat closed sessions explicitly in favor of a more streamlined translation.

Faithfulness: Operational Correspondence

Following the discussion in
Section

4.3
, here we finally show that the translation presented above satisfies the operational correctness criterion (completeness and soundness) as proposed by Gorla
[
Gor10
]
.
We first state both results, and then their proofs.

Theorem 5.1

(Completeness)
.

Given
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathtt{C}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}
, if
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\mathbin{\rightarrow}^{\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
.

Theorem 5.1

(Soundness)
.

Given
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathtt{C}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}
, if
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\mathbin{\rightarrow^{\ast}}Q
, then there exists
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D}
and
Q\mathbin{\rightarrow^{\ast}}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
.

Both results rely on the following lemma, which decomposes translations of terms/configurations under contexts into some evaluation context containing the translation of the respective term/configuration, and transfers free variables/names and substitutions in and out of the translation (cf.
Section

A.4
):

Lemma 5.2
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{R}}\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}]}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}[{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}]
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E}},z^{\prime}
;

•

{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{F}}\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}]}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}[{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}]
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E}},z^{\prime}
;

•

{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{G}}\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}]}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}[{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}]
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E}},z^{\prime}
;

•

{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}\notin\fv({\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C})
implies
x\notin\fn({\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z})
;

•

{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}\in\fv({\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C})
implies
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C\{y/x\}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}={\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\{y/x\}
.

Proof 5.3

(Proof (Sketch))
.

The first three items follow by induction on the structure of the contexts.
In the base case, the context is a hole and the thesis follows immediately.
The inductive cases follow by construction of the translation and IH.
The last two items follow by induction on the structure of the configuration and construction of the translation.

Completeness additionally relies on the following lemma, that decomposes the translation of buffers of several shapes into an evaluation context containing the translation of a continuation of the respective buffer:

Proof 5.5

(Proof (Sketch))
.

Each item follows by induction on the size of
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\vec{m}}
, and a case analysis on the shape of
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S}
.
In the base case,
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\vec{m}}={\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\epsilon}
and the thesis follows immediately.
In the inductive cases, the thesis follows by IH and the construction of the translation.

With these prerequisites in place, we move on to discuss the proofs of
Theorems

14
and
15
.

(
\ast_{3}

Rule
[

{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(\bm{\nu}x[\epsilon\rangle y)C}\equiv_{\mathtt{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(\bm{\nu}y[\epsilon\rangle x)C}

Both directions are analogous; we detail the left to right direction.
We first infer the typing of the left configuration:

\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\emptyset}\vdash_{\mathtt{B}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\epsilon\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathtt{C}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-0.99814pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to114.89684pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.24812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize{{[{typ-res/-buf}]}}}}\vskip-0.99812pt\hbox{\hskip 17.06433pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Gamma^{\prime}}\vdash_{\mathtt{C}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(\bm{\nu}x[\epsilon\rangle y)C}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}T}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\end{array}

Here,
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Gamma^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Gamma}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}y}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\overline{S}}
.
The analysis depends on whether
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}={\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\square}
and/or
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}y}={\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\square}
.
In each case, we show that

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\emptyset}\vdash_{\mathtt{B}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\epsilon\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{a\rangle b}={\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\emptyset}\vdash_{\mathtt{B}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\epsilon\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\overline{S}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\overline{S^{\prime}}}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{b\rangle a}:

(10)

–

Case
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}={\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}y}={\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\square}
, or
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\square}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\square}
.
Either way,
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S}
.
If
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\square}
, both translations are
\bm{0}
, from which the thesis follows immediately.
Otherwise, the thesis follows by induction on the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}
; clearly, the resulting translations are exactly the same.

–

Case
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}={\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\square}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}y}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\square}
, or
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\square}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}y}={\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\square}
.
W.l.o.g., assume the former.
Then
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\square}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S}={\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\mathsf{end}}
.
The thesis then holds as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\emptyset}\vdash_{\mathtt{B}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\epsilon\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\square}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\mathsf{end}}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{a\rangle b}

\displaystyle=b(\_,c);c[\_,\_]

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\emptyset}\vdash_{\mathtt{B}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\epsilon\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\mathsf{end}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\square}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{b\rangle c}

The thesis then holds as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(\bm{\nu}x[\epsilon\rangle y)C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}

\displaystyle=(\bm{\nu}ax)(\bm{\nu}by)({\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\emptyset}\vdash_{\mathtt{B}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\epsilon\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{a\rangle b}\mathbin{|}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z})

\displaystyle=(\bm{\nu}ax)(\bm{\nu}by)({\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\emptyset}\vdash_{\mathtt{B}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\epsilon\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\overline{S}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\overline{S^{\prime}}}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{b\rangle a}\mathbin{|}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z})

(
10
)

\displaystyle\equiv(\bm{\nu}by)(\bm{\nu}ax)({\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\emptyset}\vdash_{\mathtt{B}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\epsilon\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\overline{S}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\overline{S^{\prime}}}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{b\rangle a}\mathbin{|}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(\bm{\nu}y[\epsilon\rangle x)C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}

Proof 5.7

(Proof of soundness (
Theorem

15
))
.

By induction on the number
k
of steps
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\mathbin{\rightarrow}^{k}Q
(IH
1
).
We distinguish cases on all possible initial reductions
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q_{0}
and discuss all possible following reductions.
Here, we rely on
APCP
’s confluence of independent reductions, allowing us to focus on a specific sequence of reductions, postponing other possibilities that eventually lead to the same result.

We then use induction on the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}
(IH
2
).
The goal is to identify some
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D_{0}}
such that we can isolate
k_{0}\geq 0
reductions such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D_{0}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q_{0}\mathbin{\rightarrow}^{k_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D_{0}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
(where
k_{0}
may be different in each case).
We then have
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D_{0}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\mathbin{\rightarrow}^{k-k_{0}}Q
, so it follows from IH
1
that there exists
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D_{0}}\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D_{0}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\mathbin{\rightarrow^{\ast}}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
.
Here, we detail only the interesting case of an interaction between the translations of a buffer and a contained configuration;
Section

A.4.2
details the whole proof.
That is,
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}={\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(\bm{\nu}x[\vec{m}\rangle y)C_{1}}
.
We have

{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(\bm{\nu}x[\vec{m}\rangle y)C_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}=(\bm{\nu}a_{1}x)(\bm{\nu}b_{1}y)({\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\vec{m}\rangle}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{a_{1}\rangle b_{1}}\mathbin{|}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}).

The reduction may originate from (i)
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\vec{m}\rangle}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{a_{1}\rangle b_{1}}
, (ii)
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
, (iii) a synchronization between
a_{1}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\vec{m}\rangle}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{a_{1}\rangle b_{1}}
and
x
in
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
, or (iv) a synchronization between
b_{1}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\vec{m}\rangle}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{a_{1}\rangle b_{1}}
and
y
in
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
.
We detail each case:

(i)

The reduction originates from
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\vec{m}\rangle}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{a_{1}\rangle b_{1}}
.
No matter what
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\vec{m}\rangle}
is, no reduction is possible.

(ii)

The reduction originates from
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
.
We thus have
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q_{1}
.
By IH
2
, there are
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D_{1}},k_{1}\geq 0
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D_{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q_{1}\mathbin{\rightarrow}^{k_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D_{0}}\mathrel{\triangleq}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(\bm{\nu}x[\vec{m}\rangle y)D_{1}}
.
We have
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D_{0}}
.
Moreover:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}

\displaystyle=(\bm{\nu}a_{1}x)(\bm{\nu}b_{1}y)({\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\vec{m}\rangle}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{a_{1}\rangle b_{1}}\mathbin{|}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z})

\displaystyle\mathbin{\rightarrow}^{k_{1}+1}(\bm{\nu}a_{1}x)(\bm{\nu}b_{1}y)({\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\vec{m}\rangle}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{a_{1}\rangle b_{1}}\mathbin{|}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D_{0}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}

(iii)

The reduction originates from a synchronization between
a_{1}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\vec{m}\rangle}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{a_{1}\rangle b_{1}}
and
x
in
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
.
By well typedness,
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Delta}\vdash_{\mathtt{B}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\vec{m}\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S}
.
Note first that, by
Lemma

5.4
,
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\square}
implies that there are
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E_{2}}},c_{1}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\vec{m}\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\right\rrbracket}{a_{1}\rangle b_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\mathpzc{E_{2}}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\big[{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\epsilon\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\right\rrbracket}{a_{1}\rangle c_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5664,0.6133}\big]
.
The analysis depends on
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}
, so we consider all possibilities.
In each case, if the reduction is indeed possible, we show that the reduction is the first step in the execution of some rule such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D_{0}}
.
The corresponding reduction
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q_{0}\mathbin{\rightarrow}^{k_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D_{0}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
follows the corresponding case in the proof of
Theorem

14
(
Theorem 5.1

(Completeness)
.
).

•

Case
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\square}
.
Then
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}={\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\square}
is not free in
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}
, and thus
x
is not free in
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
: the reduction is not possible.

•

Case
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\mathsf{end}}
.
The analysis depends on whether
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S}={\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\square}
or not; w.l.o.g., assume not.
We have

{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\epsilon\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\mathsf{end}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\mathsf{end}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\right\rrbracket}{a_{1}\rangle c_{1}}=a_{1}(\_,c_{2});\ldots\mathbin{|}\ldots

Thus, the synchronization is between the receive on
a_{1}
and a send on
x
in
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
.

A send on a variable
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}
can only occur in the translation of that variable directly, under some reduction context.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}
is of type
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\mathsf{end}}
and its translation appears under a reduction context, the only well-typed way for
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}
to appear in
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}
is if
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{G}}\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\big[\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{F}}\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{close}}x;M_{1}\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}]\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\big]
.
We then have

{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\equiv_{\mathtt{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{G^{\prime}}}\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\big[\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(\bm{\nu}x[\vec{m}\rangle y)({\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{F}}\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{close}}x;M_{1}\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}]\mathbin{|}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{2}})\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\big].

Hence, the observed reduction is the first step of executing Rule
[
red-close
]
.

•

Case
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}{!}T_{2}.S^{\prime}_{2}}
.
We have

{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\epsilon\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}{!}T_{2}.S^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}{!}T_{2}.S^{\prime}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\right\rrbracket}{a_{1}\rangle c_{1}}=a_{1}(\_,c_{2});\ldots

Thus, the synchronization is between the receive on
a_{1}
and a send on
x
in
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
.

A send on a variable
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}
can only occur in the translation of that variable directly, under some reduction context.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}
is of type
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}{!}T_{2}.S^{\prime}_{2}}
and its translation appears under a reduction context, the only well-typed way for
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}
to appear in
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}
is if
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{G}}\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\big[\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{F}}\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{send}}M_{1}\,x\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}]\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\big]
.
We then have

{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\equiv_{\mathtt{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{G^{\prime}}}\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\big[\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(\bm{\nu}x[\vec{m}\rangle y)({\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{\hat{F^{\prime}}}}\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{send}}M_{1}\,x\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}]\mathbin{|}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{2}})\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\big].

Hence, the observed reduction is the first step of executing Rule
[

•

Case
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}{?}T_{2}.S^{\prime}_{2}}
.
We have

{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\epsilon\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}{?}T_{2}.S^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}{?}T_{2}.S^{\prime}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\right\rrbracket}{a_{1}\rangle c_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\epsilon\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}{!}T_{2}.\overline{S^{\prime}_{2}}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}{!}T_{2}.\overline{S^{\prime}_{2}}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\right\rrbracket}{c_{1}\rangle a_{1}}=c_{1}(\_,c_{2});\ldots

Thus, the reduction is not possible.

•

Case
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}{\oplus}\{i:S^{i}_{2}\}_{i\in I}}
.
We have

{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\epsilon\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}{\oplus}\{i:S^{i}_{2}\}_{i\in I}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}{\oplus}\{i:S^{i}_{2}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\right\rrbracket}{a_{1}\rangle c_{1}}=a_{1}(\_,c_{2});\ldots

Thus, the synchronization is between the receive on
a_{1}
and a send on
x
in
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
.

A send on a variable
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}
can only occur in the translation of that variable directly, under some reduction context.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}
is of type
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}{\oplus}\{i:S^{i}_{2}\}_{i\in I}}
and its translation appears under a reduction context, the only well-typed way for
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}x}
to appear in
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}
is if
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{G}}\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\big[\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{F}}\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{select}}j\,x\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}]\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\big]
where
j\in I
.
We then have

{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\equiv_{\mathtt{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{G^{\prime}}}\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\big[\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(\bm{\nu}x[\vec{m}\rangle y)({\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathpzc{F}}\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{select}}j\,x\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}]\mathbin{|}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{2}})\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\big].

Hence, the observed reduction is the first step of executing Rule
[
red-select
]
.

•

Case
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\&\{i:S^{i}_{2}\}_{i\in I}}
.
We have

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\epsilon\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\&\{i:S^{i}_{2}\}_{i\in I}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\&\{i:S^{i}_{2}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\right\rrbracket}{a_{1}\rangle c_{1}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\epsilon\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}{\oplus}\{i:\overline{S^{i}_{2}}\}_{i\in I}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}{\oplus}\{i:\overline{S^{i}_{2}}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\right\rrbracket}{c_{1}\rangle a_{1}}

\displaystyle=c_{1}(\_,c_{2});\ldots

Thus, the reduction is not possible.

(iv)

The reduction originates from a synchronization between
b_{1}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\vec{m}\rangle}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{a_{1}\rangle b_{1}}
and

in
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
.
By well typedness,
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\Delta}\vdash_{\mathtt{B}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}[\vec{m}\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S^{\prime}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S}
.
The analysis depends on
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S}
, so we consider all possibilities.
In each case, if the reduction is indeed possible, we show that the reduction is the first step in the execution of some rule such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D_{0}}
.
The corresponding reduction
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q_{0}\mathbin{\rightarrow}^{k_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D_{0}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
follows the corresponding case in the proof of
Theorem

14
(
Theorem 5.1

(Completeness)
.
).

•

Case
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}S}={\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\square}
.
Then
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}y}={\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\square}
is not free in
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C_{1}}
, and thus

is not free in

: the reduction is not possible.

above.

5.3.
Deadlock Free
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}

By virtue of
Theorem

10
,
well-typed
APCP
processes that are typable under empty contexts (
\vdash P::\emptyset
) are deadlock free.
We may transfer this result to
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
configurations by appealing to the operational correctness of our translation (
Theorems

14
and
15
above).
Each deadlock-free configuration in
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
obtained via this transference satisfies two requirements:

•

The configuration is typable
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\emptyset}\vdash_{\mathtt{C}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\mathchoice{\scriptstyle}{\scriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}{}\blacklozenge}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\bm{1}}
, i.e., it needs no external resources and has no external behavior.

•

The typed translation of the configuration satisfies priority requirements in
APCP
: it is well typed under ‘
\vdash
’, not only under ‘
\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\ast}
’ (cf.
Section

5.2
).

We rely on soundness (
Theorem

15
) to transfer deadlock freedom to configurations:

Theorem 16

(Deadlock Freedom for
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
)
.

Given
{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\emptyset}\vdash_{\mathtt{C}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\mathchoice{\scriptstyle}{\scriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}{}\blacklozenge}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}:{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\bm{1}}
, if
\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}::\Gamma
for some
\Gamma
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\mathchoice{\scriptstyle}{\scriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}{}\blacklozenge}\,()}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D}
.

Proof 5.8
.

By
Theorem

13
(type preservation for the translation),
\Gamma=z:{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.7422,0.2891,0}\bm{1}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}=z:\bullet
.
Then
\vdash(\bm{\nu}z\_){\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}::\emptyset
.
Hence, by
Theorem

10
(deadlock freedom), either
(\bm{\nu}z\_){\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\equiv\bm{0}
or
(\bm{\nu}z\_){\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q
for some
Q
.
The rest of the analysis depends on which possibility holds:

•

We have
(\bm{\nu}z\_){\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\equiv\bm{0}
.
We straightforwardly deduce from the well typedness and translation of
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}
that
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\equiv_{\mathtt{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\mathchoice{\scriptstyle}{\scriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}{}\blacklozenge}\,()}
, proving the thesis.

•

We have
(\bm{\nu}z\_){\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q
for some
Q
.
We argue that this implies that
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q_{0}
, for some
Q_{0}
.

–

The reduction involves
z
.
Since
z
is of type
\bullet
in
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
, then
z
must occur in a forwarder, and the reduction involves a forwarder
[z\leftrightarrow x]
for some
x
.
Since
x
is not free in
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
, there must be a restriction on
x
.
Hence, the forwarder
[z\leftrightarrow x]
can also reduce with this restriction, instead of with the restriction
(\bm{\nu}z\_)
.
This means that
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q_{0}
for some
Q_{0}
.

–

The reduction does not involve
z
, in which case the reduction must be internal to
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
.
That is,
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q_{0}
for some
Q_{0}
and
Q\equiv(\bm{\nu}z\_)Q_{0}
.

First, by contradiction, we show that
z
is not involved in this reduction:
Since
z
is of type
\bullet
in
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
, then
z
must occur in a forwarder, and the reduction involves a forwarder
[z\leftrightarrow x]
for some
x
.
However, our translation (
Figures

17
,
18
and
19
) does not generate forwarders, so this is clearly a contradiction.
Hence, the reduction does not involve
z
.

Thus, the reduction must be internal to
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}
.
That is,
{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}\mathbin{\rightarrow}Q_{0}
for some
Q_{0}
and
Q\equiv(\bm{\nu}z\_)Q_{0}
.
Then, by
Theorem

15
(soundness), there exists
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{C}}^{\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D}
.
Looking at the proof of
Theorem

15
(in
Section

A.4.2
), it is easy to see that in fact
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{C}}^{+}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D}
.
That is, there exists
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D_{0}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{C}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}D_{0}}
, proving the thesis.

{exa}

To illustrate the insights gained from deadlock analysis in
APCP
through translation from
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
, we consider a seemingly deadlock-free configuration that is not.
To this end, we first discuss how we can assign priorities to the translated types of the configuration, and extract from the translation requirements on those priorities.
Next, we show that our example leads to unsatisfiable priority requirements, indicating the elusive deadlock in the original configuration.
We close the example by considering an alternative configuration that is indeed deadlock free.

Our example is a classic showcase of deadlock due to cyclic connections under synchronous communication.
Though one would expect this example to be deadlock free under asynchronous communication, it is not: there is a deadlock induced by the call-by-name semantics of
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
.
Let

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M(x,y)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathinner{\mathtt{let}}x_{1}=\mathinner{\mathtt{send}}()\,x\mathinner{\mathtt{in}}\\
\mathinner{\mathtt{let}}(v,y_{1})=\mathinner{\mathtt{recv}}y\mathinner{\mathtt{in}}\\
\mathinner{\mathtt{close}}x_{1};\mathinner{\mathtt{close}}y_{1};v,\end{array}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\triangleq}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}{\mathchoice{\scriptstyle}{\scriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}{}\blacklozenge}\,\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathinner{\mathtt{let}}(x,x^{\prime})=\mathinner{\mathtt{new}}\mathinner{\mathtt{in}}\\
\mathinner{\mathtt{let}}(y,y^{\prime})=\mathinner{\mathtt{new}}\mathinner{\mathtt{in}}\\
\mathinner{\mathtt{fork}}M(x,y);M(y^{\prime},x^{\prime}).\end{array}}

Before showing the deadlock and its source, we first analyze the deadlock through translation into
APCP
.

By
Theorem

13
and assigning priority variables to each connective, we have

\displaystyle\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M(x,y)}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z_{1}}::\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}y:\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\circ_{1}}(\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\circ_{2}}\bullet)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\circ_{3}}\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\circ_{4}}\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\circ_{5}}\bullet,\\
x:\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\circ_{6}}\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\circ_{7}}(\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\circ_{8}}\bullet)\mathbin{\otimes}^{\circ_{9}}\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\circ_{10}}\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\circ_{11}}\bullet,z_{1}:\bullet\end{array}

\displaystyle\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M(y^{\prime},x^{\prime})}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z_{2}}::\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}x^{\prime}:\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{1}}(\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{2}}\bullet)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\pi_{3}}\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{4}}\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\pi_{5}}\bullet,\\
y^{\prime}:\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{6}}\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\pi_{7}}(\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\pi_{8}}\bullet)\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{9}}\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{10}}\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\pi_{11}}\bullet,z_{2}:\bullet\end{array}

\displaystyle\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z}::z:\bullet

To determine requirements on these priorities, we analyze the translations of typing rules into
APCP
derivations in
Figures

17
,
18
and
19
and the priority requirements induced by them.
Indeed, a deadlock is detected, because
APCP
requires
\circ_{6}<\pi_{1}<\pi_{6}<\circ_{1}<\circ_{6}
, which is unsatisfiable.

Before detailing the origin of these requirements, we first give an intuition behind this unsatisfiable chain of priority requirements, and how they reflect the deadlock that is indeed present in
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}
.
From left to right, the requirements denote that (1) the send on
x
occurs before the receive on
x^{\prime}
, (2) the receive on
x^{\prime}
occurs before the send on
y^{\prime}
, (3) the send on
y^{\prime}
occurs before the receive on
y
, (4) the receive on
y
occurs before the send on
x
.
Steps (2) and (3) stand out:
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M(x,y)}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M(y^{\prime},x^{\prime})}
seem to define an opposite order.
This is because in, e.g.,
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M(x,y)}
, the call-by-name semantics of
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
transforms the
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{let}}x_{1}}
into an explicit substitution that can only be resolved—thus enabling the
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{send}}()\,x}
—once the
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{close}}x_{1}}
is unblocked by the
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{let}}(v,y_{1})}
, which in turn is waiting for the
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{recv}}y}
to be resolved.
To be precise:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M(x,y)}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{M}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\big(\mathinner{\mathtt{let}}(v,y_{1})=\mathinner{\mathtt{recv}}y\mathinner{\mathtt{in}}\ldots\big){\lBrace\mathinner{\mathtt{send}}()\,x/x_{1}\rBrace}}\mathrel{\triangleq}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M^{\prime}(x,y)}\not\mkern-6.0mu\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{M}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M(y^{\prime},x^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{M}}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\big(\mathinner{\mathtt{let}}(v^{\prime},x^{\prime}_{1})=\mathinner{\mathtt{recv}}x^{\prime}\mathinner{\mathtt{in}}\ldots\big){\lBrace\mathinner{\mathtt{send}}()\,y^{\prime}/y^{\prime}_{1}\rBrace}}\mathrel{\triangleq}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M^{\prime}(y^{\prime},x^{\prime})}\not\mkern-6.0mu\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{M}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{C}}^{9}{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}(\bm{\nu}s[\epsilon\rangle s^{\prime})(\bm{\nu}t[\epsilon\rangle t^{\prime})\big({\mathchoice{\scriptstyle}{\scriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}{}\lozenge}\,M^{\prime}(x,t){\lBrace s/x\rBrace}\parallel{\mathchoice{\scriptstyle}{\scriptstyle}{\scriptscriptstyle}{}\blacklozenge}\,M^{\prime}(y^{\prime},s^{\prime}){\lBrace t^{\prime}/y^{\prime}\rBrace}\big)}\not\mkern-6.0mu\mathbin{\rightarrow}_{\mathtt{C}}

We now detail the origin of the conflicting priority requirements; that is, we spell out the rules of the translation and the induced requirements.
Note that many of these rules include names and priority variables that are not visible outside the derivation, being created from within certain parts of the translation.

(1)

Requirement
\circ_{6}<\pi_{1}
originates from the translation of
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{new}}}
in the
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{let}}(x,x^{\prime})}
.
More precisely, the translation of
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{new}}}
prepares the translation of the upcoming buffer, which follows the involved types.
The requirement is induced by a step in the translation of this buffer (Rule [

::\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}x^{\prime}:\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{1}}(\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{2}}\bullet)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\pi_{3}}\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{4}}\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\pi_{5}}\bullet,\\
a^{\prime}_{1}:(\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{12}}\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\pi_{11}}\bullet)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\pi_{13}}\bullet\end{array}\end{array}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{send}}()\,y^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{e^{\prime}_{1}}\\
::\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}y^{\prime}:\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{6}}\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\pi_{7}}(\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\pi_{8}}\bullet)\\
{}\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{9}}\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{10}}\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\pi_{11}}\bullet,\end{array}\\
e^{\prime}_{1}:\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{11}}\bullet\end{array}\end{array}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\underline{\pi_{12}<\pi_{6}},\\
\pi_{13}<\pi_{12}\end{array}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to836.96172pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 96.57611pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\ast}{\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{let}}y^{\prime}_{1}=\mathinner{\mathtt{send}}y^{\prime}\mathinner{\mathtt{in}}\ldots}\color[rgb]{0.6055,0,0.0117}\rrbracket}{z_{2}}::\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}x^{\prime}:\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{1}}(\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{2}}\bullet)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\pi_{3}}\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{4}}\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\pi_{5}}\bullet,\\
y^{\prime}:\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{6}}\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\pi_{7}}(\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\pi_{8}}\bullet)\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{9}}\bullet\mathbin{\otimes}^{\pi_{10}}\bullet\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\&}$}}{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\&}$}}}^{\pi_{11}}\bullet,\\
z_{2}:\bullet\end{array}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\end{array}

(3)

Requirement
\pi_{6}<\circ_{1}
originates from the buffer prepared by the translation of
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{new}}}
in the
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{let}}(y,y^{\prime})}
.
It is derived similar to Requirement (1).

(4)

Requirement
\circ_{1}<\circ_{6}
originates from four similar substeps as in Requirement (2).

We conclude that the translation of
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}
cannot be typed under
\vdash
, because the priority requirements cannot be satisfied.
Hence,
16
does not apply, and so deadlock freedom cannot be guaranteed for
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}
.

Note that it is straightforward to define variants of
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}C}
whose translations are well typed under
\vdash
, thus guaranteeing deadlock freedom through
16
.
An example is the variant of
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}M(x,y)}
above in which the
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{close}}x_{1}}
occurs before the
{\color[rgb]{0.0977,0,0.543}\mathinner{\mathtt{let}}(v,y_{1})}
.

Remark 17
.

Determining the deadlock freedom of well-typed
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
programs by means of translation into
APCP
is of similar complexity as a direct, priority-based approach.
It is easy to see that the translation is
\mathpzc{O}(n)
in the size of the
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
typing derivation.
We could add priorities to the types of
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
and derive priority requirements from the translation; this would boil down to roughly the same amount of checking.
Hence, the indirect approach via
APCP
is similarly complex.
This justifies keeping the type system of
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
simple, compared to type systems that use priorities for deadlock-free functional programs such as those by, e.g., Padovani and Novara
[
PN15
]
and Kokke and Dardha
[
KD21a
]
.

6.
Related Work

Closely related work on the two themes of the paper (deadlock freedom by typing and functional calculi with concurrency) has been already discussed throughout the paper. Here we comment on other related literature.

Asynchronous Communication

Asynchronous communication has a longstanding history in process algebras (see, e.g.,
[
BKT85
,
HJH90
,
BKP92
]
).
The first accounts of asynchronous communication for the
\pi
-calculus were developed independently by Honda and Tokoro
[
HT91
,
HT92
]
and by Boudol
[
Bou92
]
.
Palamidessi
[
Pal03
]
shows that the synchronous
\pi
-calculus is strictly more expressive than its asynchronous variant, due to
mixed choices
: non-deterministic choices involving both inputs and outputs.
Beauxis
et al.

[
BPV08
]
study the exact form of asynchronous communication modeled by the asynchronous
\pi
-calculus; they examine communication mediated by different mechanisms (bags, stacks, queues) in the synchronous
\pi
-calculus, and prove that bags lead to the strongest operational correspondence with the asynchronous
\pi
-calculus.

As discussed already, asynchrony is a relevant phenomenon in session
\pi
-calculi; the communication structures delineated by sessions strongly influence the expected ordering of messages.
The expressiveness gap between asynchronous and synchronous communication shown by Palamidessi in the untyped setting does not hold in this context, since session-typed
\pi
-calculi consider only deterministic choices and do not account for mixed choices.
A notable exception is the work on
mixed sessions
by Casal
et al.

[
VCAM20
,
CMV22
]
, which does not address deadlock freedom.
Nevertheless, fundamental differences between mixed choices in untyped and session-typed settings remain, as established by Peters and Yoshida
[
PY22
]
.

In the context of session types, the first formal theory of asynchronous communication for the
\pi
-calculus is by Kouzapas
et al.

[
KYH11
]
, using a buffered semantics.
Their focus is on the behavioral theory induced by asynchrony and the program transformations it enables.
Follow-up work
[
KYHH16
]
goes beyond to consider asynchronous communication in combination with constructs for event-driven programming, and develops a corresponding type system and behavioral theory.
Unlike our work, these two works do not consider deadlock freedom for asynchronous session processes.

DeYoung
et al.

[
DCPT12
]
give the first connection between linear logic and a session-typed
\pi
-calculus with asynchronous communication using a continuation-passing semantics, and show that this semantics is equivalent to a buffered semantics.
As mentioned before,
APCP
’s semantics is based on this work, except that our typing rules for sending and selection are axiomatic and that we consider recursion.
On a similar line, Jia
et al.

[
JGP16
]
consider a session-typed language with asynchronous communication; their focus is on the dynamic monitoring of session behaviors, not on deadlock freedom.
Both these works are based on the correspondence with intuitionistic linear logic, which restricts the kind of process networks allowed by typing.
Pruiksma and Pfenning
[
PP19
,
PP21
]
derive a “propositions-as-sessions” type system from adjoint logic, which combines several logics with different structural rules through modalities
[
Ree09
,
PCPR18
]
.
Their process language features asynchronous communication with continuation passing and their type system treats asynchronous, non-blocking outputs via axiomatic typing rules, similar to Rules [
typ-send
] and [
typ-sel
] in
Figure

4
.

Padovani’s linear type system for the asynchronous
\pi
-calculus
[
Pad14
]
has been already mentioned in the context of deadlock freedom for cyclic process networks.
His language is different from
APCP
, as it lacks session constructs and does not have continuation passing baked into the type system.
While it should be possible to encode sessions in his typed framework (using communication of pairs to model continuation passing), it seems unclear how to transfer the analysis of deadlock freedom from this setting to a language such as
APCP
via such a translation.

Deadlock Freedom for Cyclic Process Networks

We have already discussed the related works by Kobayashi
[
Kob06
]
, Padovani
[
Pad14
]
, and Dardha and Gay
[
DG18
]
.
The work of Kobayashi and Laneve
[
KL17
]
is related to
APCP
in that it addresses deadlock freedom for
unbounded
process networks.
Toninho and Yoshida’s work
[
TY18
]
addresses deadlock freedom for cyclic process networks by generating global types from binary types.
The work by Balzer, Toninho and Pfenning
[
BP17
,
BTP19
]
is also worth mentioning: it guarantees deadlock freedom for processes with shared, mutable resources by means of manifest sharing, i.e., explicitly acquiring and releasing access to resources.

Functional Languages with Sessions

We have already discussed the related works by Gay and Vasconcelos
[
GV10
]
, Wadler
[
Wad12
]
, Kokke and Dardha
[
KD21a
,
KD21b
]
, and Padovani and Novara
[
PN15
]
.
Lindley and Morris
[
LM15
]
formally define a semantics for Wadler’s
GV
, prove deadlock freedom and operational correspondence of the translation into Wadler’s
CP
, and give a translation from
CP
into
GV
.
Other related work in this line is Fowler
et al.
’s
Asynchronous

GV
with buffered asynchronous communication and a call-by-value semantics, and
Exceptional

GV
(
EGV
) which extends the former with exception handling
[
Fow19
,
FLMD19
]
.

Table

1
summarizes the comparison of
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
to some of these related works.
Note that none of the mentioned works supports sending anything but values, unlike
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
.
Our work ensures deadlock freedom for configurations with cyclic topologies by means of a translation into a system with an established deadlock-freedom result (cf.
17
); the alternative approach is to enhance
GV
’s type system with priorities, as done by Padovani and Novara
[
PN15
]
and Kokke and Dardha
[
KD21a
,
KD21b
]
.

)

Table 1.
The features of
CGV
compared to related works.

Typed Encodings between (Concurrent)
\lambda
-calculi and
\pi
-calculi

Several prior works develop typed encodings between
\lambda
- and
\pi
-calculi; to our knowledge, all of them consider call-by-value semantics, most do not consider
\lambda
with message passing, and none translate variables as sends, as we do.
Some of these works have been already discussed in
Section

4.3
, where we justified the notions of completeness and soundness relevant for our translation of
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
into
APCP
.

Vasconcelos
[
Vas00
]
translates an untyped
\lambda
-calculus into an input/output-typed
\pi
-calculus; the translation is sound, up to barbed congruence.
The aforementioned work by Wadler
[
Wad12
,
Wad14
]
translates the session-typed
GV
with synchronous message passing into the session-typed
CP
, without giving a semantics for
GV
nor associated completeness/soundness results.
Lindley and Morris
[
LM15
]
formalize a semantics for
GV
and its translation into
CP
, and prove completeness.
Similarly to our approach, they use closures/explicit substitutions in
GV
to obtain a direct, more controlled correspondence with cut reduction in linear logic; however, no soundness result is given.
Kokke and Dardha
[
KD21a
]
define
PGV
, an extension of
GV
with priority annotations to support deadlock-free cyclic thread configurations; they give a translation from
PCP
into
PGV
that is both sound and complete, but no translation in the other direction is studied.
Toninho
et al.

[
TCP12
]
translate a linearly typed
\lambda
-calculus into a session-typed
\pi
-calculus derived from intuitionistic linear logic; they prove completeness, but for soundness they extend reduction in
\lambda
to call by name.
Toninho and Yoshida
[
TY21
]
translate a linearly typed
\lambda
-calculus without message passing into a session-typed polymorphic
\pi
-calculus. Their translation is fully abstract: it is complete and sound, with the latter result requiring an extension of reduction in
\pi
with commuting conversions.
Fowler
et al.

[
FKD+23
]

introduce a variant of (call-by-value)
GV
based on
hypersequents
, and
consider its relationship with hypersequent
CP
: they exhibit translations that preserve and reflect reduction, up to weak bisimilarity.

Other Applications of
APCP

As mentioned in the introduction, two salient aspects of Curry-Howard correspondences for session types, namely analysis of deadlock freedom and connections with functional calculi, define the main themes of this paper.
Yet another highlight of the logical correspondences is their suitability for the analysis of
multiparty protocols
.
In separate work
[
HP22b
]
, we have devised a methodology for the analysis of multiparty session types (MPST) based on
APCP

[
HYC16
]
.
In the multiparty context, two or more participants interact following a common protocol.

APCP
is well suited for the analysis of process implementations of MPST, which rely on asynchrony and recursion.
In our analysis, multiple separate processes implement the roles of one or more participants of a multiparty protocol.
The support for cyclic process networks in
APCP
allows us to connect these implementations with each other directly, without requiring an additional process (as used in similar prior works
[
CP16
,
CMSY15
,
CLM+16
]
).
The asynchrony in
APCP
captures the asynchronous nature of MPST, and
APCP
’s recursion enables an expressive class of supported protocols.
This way, our methodology unlocks the transfer of (static) analysis of deadlock freedom and protocol conformance from
APCP
to distributed implementations of MPST.
The key ideas underlying the methodology in
[
HP22b
]
can be applied to the
runtime
verification of such distributed implementations, as recently shown in
[
HPD23
]
.

7.
Closing Remarks

In this paper, we have presented two contributions to the challenging issue of ensuring deadlock-free, message-passing interactions in a session-typed setting.
Our
first contribution
is
APCP
: a typed framework for deadlock-free cyclic process networks with asynchronous communication and recursion.
The design of
APCP
and its type system has a solid basis, with tight ties to logic (“propositions as sessions”): our syntax, semantics and type system harmoniously integrate insights separately presented in prior works for different typed variants of the
\pi
-calculus.

The step from synchronous communication (as in
PCP

[
DG18
]
) to asynchronous communication (in
APCP
) is significant.
In combination with cyclic process networks, asynchronous communication enables checking a larger class of deadlock-free processes: processes that under synchronous communication would be deadlocked due to blocking outputs may be deadlock free in
APCP
(see, e.g.,
Section

3.1
).
Perhaps more significantly, asynchronous communication simplifies the priority management involved in the detection of cyclic dependencies (cf.
1
).

In our
second contribution
, we move to consider cyclic process networks with asynchronous communication in the setting of a prototypical functional language.
We introduced
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
, a new functional language with asynchronous, session-typed communication.
Inspired by Gay and Vasconcelos’s
LAST
, the design of
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
combines buffered communication with a call-by-name reduction strategy, explicit substitutions, and explicit session closing.
In our opinion, this design makes
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
an interesting object of study on its own.
In the spirit of Gay and Vasconcelos’s work, we defined a deliberately simple type system for
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
, whose type preservation property ensures protocol fidelity and communication safety, but not deadlock freedom.
This means that well-typed threads in
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
can perform session protocols that may lead to stuck terms/configurations.
To connect our contributions, and to transfer the deadlock-freedom guarantee to the functional realm, we presented a translation from
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
to
APCP
that is
operationally correct
.
In particular, operational correctness includes the
soundness
property, which ensures that the translation reflects reduction steps and is critical to the transfer of deadlock freedom formalized by
16
.
This way, we were able to analyze deadlock freedom in
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
by leaning on the established results for
APCP
(
Theorem

10
) without sacrificing complexity (cf.
17
).

In future work, it would be interesting to study the extension of
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
with recursion/recursive types, and to extend our translation into
APCP
(and its operational correspondence results) accordingly. We do not expect technical difficulties, in particular if
\texttt{LAST}^{\mathit{n}}
is enhanced with the kind of tail-recursive behavior present in
APCP
. In the same spirit, it would be insightful to explore how to accommodate
APCP
’s support for priorities into a process language with inductive and coinductive types (least and greatest fixed points, respectively), such as the one studied by Rocha and Caires
[
RC23
]
.
Finally, following
[
Kob05
]
, it would be interesting to study type inference algorithms for
APCP
which could (automatically) determine priorities for typed processes.
